Minimal Projective Resolutions, Möbius Inversion, and Bottleneck Stability
Hideto Asashiba, Amit K. Patel

Abstract
We develop a stability theory for minimal projective resolutions of 𝐏 \mathbf{P} -modules, where 𝐏 \mathbf{P} is a finite metric poset. On the module side, we introduce the Galois transport distance , defined by factoring two modules through a common “apex” poset via pairs of Galois insertions and measuring the maximal displacement in the index poset. This construction generalizes the interleaving distance in both the classical one-parameter and multiparameter settings, and yields an extended metric on isomorphism classes of 𝐏 \mathbf{P} -modules. On the homological side, we define a bottleneck distance between minimal projective resolutions by matching indecomposable projectives degreewise, with contractible cones playing the role of diagonal terms. Our main theorem shows that this resolution-level bottleneck distance is always bounded above by the Galois transport distance, providing a metric stability result formulated entirely at the level of modules and their minimal projective resolutions. We then treat persistence as an application. Passing to the interval poset and a kernel construction, we interpret persistence diagrams as minimal projective resolutions of kernel modules and obtain a corresponding stability inequality. In the one-parameter case this recovers classical bottleneck stability, while in the multiparameter setting it extends naturally to signed diagrams arising from minimal projective resolutions. Via a general relationship between minimal projective resolutions and Möbius inversion, these results can be interpreted as a stability theorem for Möbius homology, while remaining entirely phrased in the language of projective resolutions.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.
16G20, 18G10, 55N31, 62R40.

1
Introduction

Throughout the paper we fix a field
\Bbbk
, and all vector spaces and
linear maps are taken over
\Bbbk
. We write
\mathsf{vec}
for the category of
finite–dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces. For a poset
\mathbf{P}
, a

\mathbf{P}
-module
is a functor
\mathbf{P}\to\mathsf{vec}
; thus
\mathbf{P}
-modules
are precisely finite–dimensional representations of the incidence
category of
\mathbf{P}
. Their category is denoted by
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
. This
viewpoint encompasses both persistence modules in topological data
analysis and functor–valued representations studied in
representation theory.

In the one–parameter case, where
\mathbf{P}
is a finite totally ordered
poset, finitely generated persistence modules enjoy a remarkably rigid
structure: every such module decomposes as a direct sum of interval
modules, and its persistence diagram may be understood either as the
multiset of indecomposable interval summands or as a Möbius inversion
of kernel or rank functions on the interval poset

[
19
,
37
,
16
,
34
,
26
,
29
]
.
Bottleneck stability then identifies the interleaving distance with an
optimal–transport–type distance between persistence diagrams

[
16
,
13
,
14
]
.

For multiparameter persistence, this picture breaks down. A
multiparameter persistence module is again a functor
\mathbf{P}\to\mathsf{vec}
,
but now
\mathbf{P}
is typically a product of two or more finite totally ordered posets. Such modules need not
decompose into intervals, and the Möbius inversion of their kernel or
rank functions is, in general,
signed

[
26
,
29
,
2
,
3
]
.
Viewed through Möbius homology
[
33
]
and the rank–exact Betti–table
perspective, these signed coefficients naturally separate into
homological degrees, giving rise to the “signed barcodes” of
Botnan–Oppermann–Oudot–Scoccola
[
8
]
, with no
analogue in the one–parameter setting. Remark
4.2

makes this relationship precise for arbitrary
\mathbf{P}
-modules by
expressing Möbius homology in terms of minimal projective resolutions.

A central difficulty has been to formulate a satisfactory

stability theorem
in this signed, multiparameter context. In
one parameter, the bottleneck distance is a genuine metric and, by the
isometry theorem of Lesnick, coincides with the interleaving distance

[
28
]
. In contrast, natural extensions of the bottleneck
construction to signed barcodes typically fail the triangle inequality,
yielding only weak lower bounds on interleaving

[
8
]
. Related issues arise in earlier edit–distance
approaches to generalized persistence diagrams; see the clarification in

[
30
]
for the framework of

[
29
]
. Parallel work of Bubenik and
Elchesen develops Wasserstein–type metrics on virtual persistence
diagrams
[
10
]
, but a stability theory formulated
directly at the level of
\mathbf{P}
-modules and their Möbius–theoretic
invariants has remained elusive.

Main idea.

In this paper we construct a metric stability theory that operates
directly on
\mathbf{P}
-modules and their minimal projective resolutions.
Our approach has two layers.

(1)
Galois transport and resolutions.

For a finite metric poset
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
we introduce, in
Section
3
, the
Galois transport distance

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\times\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow[0,\infty],

defined by infimizing the cost of
Galois couplings
:
factorizations of modules through a common apex poset along Galois
insertions. This construction generalizes the interleaving distance, both in the classical one-parameter setting
[
13
]
and in the multiparameter setting
[
28
]
.
Independently of persistence, we then define in
Section
4
a bottleneck distance
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}
on minimal
projective resolutions, obtained by matching indecomposable summands
degreewise while allowing padding by contractible cones.

Our main theorem (Theorem
5.2
) establishes the stability
inequality

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)\qquad(M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}),

showing that Galois transport controls the optimal–transport–type
matching of minimal projective resolutions. This result stands independently of
persistence and applies to arbitrary
\mathbf{P}
-modules.

(2)
Persistence as an application.

In Section
6
we specialize this framework to the
interval poset
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
by introducing a kernel functor

K:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{P}}:=\mathbf{P}\sqcup\{\top\}
is the augmented poset of
\mathbf{P}
by adding the formal largest element (the top element)
\top
.
The minimal projective resolution

K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
provides a categorified Möbius inversion of the the module
K(M)
, equivalent to the signed persistence diagrams of
M

[
33
]
via
Remark
4.2
and the rank–exact Betti–table of
M

[
8
]
. We show that
K
is

1
–Lipschitz with respect to Galois transport, and hence obtain a
stability result for these resolution–valued persistence diagrams. In
the one–parameter case this recovers the classical bottleneck stability
theorem. Appendix
A
further shows that when

\mathbf{P}=\mathbb{R}
, the Galois transport distance coincides with the usual
interleaving distance.

1.1
Purpose

The purpose of this paper is twofold. First, at the level of arbitrary

\mathbf{P}
-modules, we construct a metric stability theory for a
categorified
Möbius inversion
, formulated in terms of minimal projective resolutions.
Second, we specialize this framework to persistence by passing to the interval
poset and the kernel construction, recovering classical bottleneck stability in
one parameter and extending it to signed multiparameter diagrams.

Minimal projective resolutions and their Betti tables have recently emerged as
central invariants for multiparameter persistence modules, both in the
rank-exact setting and in the usual multigraded setting

[
8
,
32
]
. On the computational side,
there is now a growing body of work on algorithms for computing minimal
presentations and resolutions, and hence Betti tables, for low-parameter
modules
[
27
,
17
,
12
]
. Our approach
provides a metric layer that sits above these developments: it views minimal
projective resolutions as the recipients of an optimal-transport-type
stability theorem, with categorified Möbius inversion as the organizing
principle.

Remark
4.2
explains, for a
general
\mathbf{P}
-module
X
, how its Möbius homology (in the sense of

[
33
,
21
]
) is encoded in a minimal
projective resolution
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{X}
via
\Ext
-groups, providing a formal bridge
between Möbius inversion and minimal projective resolutions. To our knowledge this
connection has not been made explicit before in this generality; a fuller treatment would merit a separate paper. The present work
leans on this bridge to phrase all our constructions and stability results in
terms of minimal projective resolutions, while still controlling Möbius-theoretic
invariants.

Concretely, for a finite metric poset
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
we depart from the
classical interleaving-distance viewpoint and instead take a
Galois-theoretic version of optimal transport. Following
Gülen–McCleary
[
22
]
, we recast interleavings in terms of
adjoint pairs of monotone maps. In Section
3
we
define the
Galois transport distance

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\times\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow[0,\infty]

(Definition
3.4
) by infimizing the cost of

Galois couplings
: factorizations of two
\mathbf{P}
-modules
M,N
through a
common apex poset
\mathbf{Q}
via two Galois insertions

f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
and
h:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:i
, together
with a module
\Gamma\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
whose pullbacks along the right adjoints
recover
M
and
N
. The cost of such a coupling is the
L^{\infty}
-type
quantity

\cost(\Gamma)\ :=\ \sup_{q\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\bigl(f(q),h(q)\bigr),

directly mirroring the Kantorovich formulation of optimal transport in which
one transports mass along a cost function and measures the worst-case
displacement
[
36
,
35
,
20
]
.
We then set
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)
to be the infimum of
\cost(\Gamma)
over all such
couplings. Proposition
3.6
shows that
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}
is an
extended pseudometric, and Corollary
3.7
upgrades it to
an extended metric on isomorphism classes of
\mathbf{P}
-modules. In
Appendix
A
we prove that when
\mathbf{P}=\mathbb{R}
with its usual
metric,
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}
recovers the classical interleaving distance, so Galois
transport refines the interleaving picture while remaining compatible with it.

On the “diagram side” we work not with signed barcodes directly, but with
minimal projective resolutions. For a finite metric poset
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
we
introduce, in Section
4
, a bottleneck distance

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}:\{P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}\}\times\{P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\}\longrightarrow[0,\infty]

between minimal projective resolutions by matching indecomposable projective
summands degreewise, measuring the distance between summands via
d_{\mathbf{P}}
on
the underlying points, and allowing padding by contractible cones that plays
the role of matching to the diagonal. Theorem
4.11

shows that this yields an extended metric on the space of minimal resolutions.
Our first stability theorem (Theorem
5.2
) shows that the Galois
transport distance controls this resolution-level bottleneck distance:

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)\qquad(M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}).

Conceptually, this inequality is obtained by a single functorial construction:
given a Galois coupling for
(M,N)
, we choose a projective resolution of the
apex module
\Gamma
, pull it back along the two right adjoints to obtain
resolutions of
M
and
N
, and then match their indecomposable summands
degreewise. Even in the one-parameter setting, this viewpoint reinterprets
the classical bottleneck distance in terms of resolutions pulled back along
Galois couplings, clarifying why many different choices of matchings (or even
different apex couplings) can induce the same bottleneck distance.

Our second main step is to connect this framework to persistence diagrams.
For a finite metric poset
\mathbf{P}
, we consider the interval
poset
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}:=\{[x,y]\mid x,y\in\overline{\mathbf{P}},x\leq y\}
of
\overline{\mathbf{P}}

with the partial order given by the product order by regarding
[x,y]

as the pair
(x,y)\in\overline{\mathbf{P}}\times\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, and define, in Section
6
, a kernel functor

K:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}

by sending an interval
[x,y]\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
to the kernel of the
structure map
M(x\to y)
, where
M(\top):=0
by convention.
Thus
K(M)
is the

kernel module
associated to
M
, viewed as a functor on the interval
poset. We show in Proposition
6.12
that
K
is
1-Lipschitz with respect to Galois transport, and we define the persistence
diagram of
M
to be the minimal projective resolution
K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
.

In the persistence setting we apply Remark
4.2
to the kernel
module
K(M)
, regarded as an
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
-module. This shows that the minimal
projective resolution
K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
encodes the Möbius homology of
K(M)
and,
via the rank-exact Betti-table viewpoint

[
8
,
32
]
, produces a signed barcode.
Combining the Lipschitz property of
K
with the resolution-level stability
inequality yields our main persistence stability result
(Theorem
6.14
):

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N).

In the one-parameter case, where
\mathbf{P}
is a finite chain in
\mathbb{R}
with a top
element adjoined and
d_{\mathbf{P}}
is the path metric, this recovers the classical
bottleneck stability theorem for persistence diagrams.

We work over
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}\cong\{[x,y]\subseteq\overline{\mathbf{P}}\mid x\leq y\}
rather than more general families of subsets
(cf.
[
26
]
or Definition
3.8
).
Even this setting already requires delicate homological control; extending the
construction further would require additional combinatorial machinery.
See Sect.
7.1
for more detail.

1.2
Outline

Section
2
collects the categorical and homological
preliminaries for
\mathbf{P}
-modules, including projective resolutions
and cones. Section
3
introduces Galois
couplings and defines the Galois transport distance
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}
on

\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, generalizing the interleaving distance.

Section
4
then defines the bottleneck distance
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}

on minimal projective resolutions and proves that it is an extended
pseudometric. In the subsequent section we combine this construction
with Galois transport to obtain our main stability theorem

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}
(Theorem
5.2
).

Section
6
treats persistence as an application.
We construct the augmented poset
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
from a finite poset
\mathbf{P}
by
adding the formal largest element, called the top element
\top
.
This is extended to a 2-functor
\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}
of the 2-category
of finite posets. Moreover, to define
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, we construct the interval poset
\Int S:=\{(x,y)\in S\times S\mid x,y\in S,x\leq y\}
as a full subposet of
S\times S
with the product order for each
S\in\mathbf{Pos}
. This is also extended to a 2-functor
\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}
.
By composing these, we have a 2-functor
\Int\circ\overline{(\operatorname{-})}\colon\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}
, which yields a new Galois insertion

\Int\overline{f}:\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}\rightleftarrows\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}:\Int\overline{g}

from a Galois insertion
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
in
\mathbf{Pos}
.
We finally construct the kernel functor

K:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
, which has an important relation
with the 2-functor
\Int\circ\overline{(\operatorname{-})}

that if
(\mathbf{Q},\,f\dashv g,\,h\dashv i,\,\Gamma)
is a Galois coupling of the pair
(M,N)
, then

(\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\,\Int\overline{f}\dashv\Int\overline{g},\,\Int\overline{h}\dashv\Int\overline{i},\,K(\Gamma))

is a Galois coupling of the pair
(K(M),K(N))
(Lemma
6.11
).
This is used to show that the GT-distance of
K(M)
and
K(N)
is
at most the GT-distance of
M
and
N
.
We interpret the minimal projective resolution of
K(M)
as a categorified Möbius inversion, and show that
K
is

1
-Lipschitz with respect to Galois transport, yielding stability for the resulting resolution-valued persistence diagrams
(Theorem
6.14
). Finally,
Appendix
A
shows that for
\mathbf{P}=\mathbb{R}
, the Galois
transport distance coincides with the usual interleaving distance.

This paper was written with the assistance of ChatGPT 5 Pro. The authors have reviewed all results and take full
responsibility for the mathematical content and any remaining errors.

2
Preliminaries

We start with some categorical facts that will be used throughout.

Lemma 2.1
:

Let
L:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be a functor between abelian categories.
If
L
has an exact right adjoint
R:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
,
then
L
sends projectives to projectives.

Proof.

Let
A
be projective in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Then the functor
{\mathcal{C}}(A,\operatorname{-})
is exact, hence so is

{\mathcal{C}}(A,\operatorname{-})\circ R={\mathcal{C}}(A,R(\operatorname{-}))\cong{\mathcal{D}}(L(A),\operatorname{-}).

Thus
L(A)
is projective.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{U}
be a universe. An element of
\mathfrak{U}
is called a
\mathfrak{U}
-
small
set. A category
{\mathcal{C}}
is
\mathfrak{U}
-
small
if its object set is
\mathfrak{U}
-small and, for any objects
X,Y
, each hom-set
{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
is
\mathfrak{U}
-small. We denote by
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}
the
2
-category whose objects are the
\mathfrak{U}
-small categories, whose
1
-morphisms are functors, and whose
2
-morphisms are natural transformations.

Proposition 2.2
:

If
{\mathcal{C}}
is an object of
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}
, then

\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\mbox{-},{\mathcal{C}}):(\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}})^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}

is a
2
-functor.

Proof.

See Proposition 4.5.4 in
[
25
]
.
∎

Remark 2.3
:

In particular, the
2
-functor
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}(\mbox{-},{\mathcal{C}})
sends an adjoint system
(f,g,\eta,\varepsilon)
to the adjoint system
(g^{*},f^{*},\eta^{*},\varepsilon^{*})
in a contravariant manner. Concretely, if
f\dashv g
between categories
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
, then precomposition yields
g^{*}\dashv f^{*}
between the functor categories
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}})
and
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathfrak{U}}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})
. We use this repeatedly with
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{vec}
below
by considering a universe
\mathfrak{U}
such that
\mathsf{vec}
is a
\mathfrak{U}
-small category.

2.1
Poset Modules

Throughout,
\Bbbk
is a field, and
\mathbf{P}=(\mathbf{P},\leq)
is a poset. We regard
\mathbf{P}
as a small (thin) category: there is a unique morphism

\pi_{y\geq x}\colon x\to y

(2.1)

exactly when
x\leq y
.
We assume
\mathbf{P}
is finite except in Appendix
A
.
Let
\mathsf{vec}
denote the category of finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces, and write
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathbf{P},\mathsf{vec})
for the category of
\mathbf{P}
-modules. If
\mathbf{P}
is finite, then
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear, abelian, Krull–Schmidt category, and for each
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
the
\Bbbk
-space
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)
is finite dimensional.

Recall that the incidence category
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]
of
\mathbf{P}

is the
\Bbbk
-linearization of the category
\mathbf{P}
.
Namely, it is a
\Bbbk
-linear category with the same objects
as
\mathbf{P}
, and
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](x,y)
is the vector space with

\mathbf{P}(x,y)
as a basis for all
x,y\in\mathbf{P}
, and the composition of
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]

is defined as the bilinearization of that of
\mathbf{P}
.
Any functor
\mathbf{P}\to\mathsf{vec}
is uniquely extended to
a
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]\to\mathsf{vec}
, and this correspondence is uniquely extended to an isomorphism
of categories from
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
to the category
\operatorname{Fun}_{\Bbbk}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}],\mathsf{vec})
of
\Bbbk
-linear functors from
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]
to
\mathsf{vec}
, by which
we may identify these categories.

Note here that the representable functor
\mathbf{P}(x,\operatorname{-})

with
x\in\mathbf{P}
is a functor from
\mathbf{P}
to the category of small sets not to
\mathsf{vec}
.
However, if we take the incidence category
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]
of
\mathbf{P}
instead of
\mathbf{P}
, then the representable functor

\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}:=\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](x,\operatorname{-})

becomes a functor
\mathbf{P}\to\mathsf{vec}
.
It sends
y\in\mathbf{P}
to
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](x,y)=\Bbbk\cdot\pi_{y\geq x}
if
x\leq y
and to
0
otherwise; for a relation
y\leq z
,

\pi_{z\geq y}
acts from the left by the identity on
\Bbbk
whenever
x\leq y
(and by
0
otherwise).
By the Yoneda lemma there is an isomorphism

\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x},M)\cong M(x)

natural in
x\in\mathbf{P}
and in
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
showing that each
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
is projective.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x},\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x})\cong\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](x,x)\cong\Bbbk
is a local algebra, the projective module
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
is indecomposable.
Again by the Yoneda lemma,
for any
\mathbf{P}
-module
L
and any
l\in L(x)
,
there exists a unique morphism

\alpha\colon\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}\to L
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
such that
\alpha(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{x})=l
.
We denote this
\alpha
by
\rho(l)
, which is defined by the “right multiplication” by
l
:
g\mapsto g(l)
for all
g\in\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](x,y)
and
y\in\mathbf{P}
.
If
I
is an interval of
\mathbf{P}
, then by definition,
V_{I}(x)=\Bbbk

for all
x\in I
. We write this
\Bbbk
as
\Bbbk=\Bbbk 1_{x}
by setting

1_{x}
to be the identity element of
\Bbbk
.
We set

\rho(y\geq x):=\rho(\pi_{y\geq x})\colon\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{y}\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
and
\rho(x):=\rho(1_{x})\colon\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}\to V_{I}

for short.
By convention, we set
\rho(y\geq x):=0
unless
y\geq x
for all
x,y\in\mathbf{P}
.
Then we have

\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{y},\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x})=\Bbbk\,\rho(y\geq x)

(2.2)

for all
x,y\in\mathbf{P}
.
These notations are used in computations of projective resolutions
such as in Example
4.12
.
We write
\operatorname{prj}\mathbf{P}
for the full subcategory of projective objects in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
. The set of nonnegative integers is denoted by
\mathbb{N}
.
Thus note that
0\in\mathbb{N}
in this paper.
Finally, if
A
is an abelian (additive) group and
B
is a set,
then
A^{B}
denotes the direct product of
B
-copies of
A
, namely the abelian group of maps
B\to A
, and we set

A^{(B)}:=\{(a_{i})_{i\in B}\in A^{B}\mid\{i\in B\mid a_{i}\neq 0\}\text{ is finite}\}
, the direct sum of
B
-copies of
A
.

For a finite sequence
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
of elements of a set
S
,
we denote by
\{\!\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\!\}
the
multiset
determined by
\boldsymbol{x}
,
which is obtained from
\boldsymbol{x}
by ignoring the order of elements.
If
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, where

a_{i}\neq a_{j}
if
i\neq j
, and
l_{i}
is the multiplicity of
a_{i}
in
\boldsymbol{x}

for each
i=1,\dots,m
, then we may identify
\{\!\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\!\}

with the set
\{(a_{i},1),\dots,(a_{i},l_{i})\mid i=1,\dots,m\}
.
By this identification we apply all notions concerning sets to any
multisets.

Lemma 2.4
:

The set
\{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}\mid x\in\mathbf{P}\}
is a complete set of representatives of the isomorphism classes of indecomposable projective
\mathbf{P}
-modules. Hence each
M\in\operatorname{prj}\mathbf{P}
decomposes as

M\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x_{i}}

for a unique
|M|:=n\in\mathbb{N}
and a unique multiset

\Summands(M)=\{\!\{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x_{1}},\dots,\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x_{n}}\}\!\}
, which are called
the
size
and the
summand set
of
M
, respectively.
(By convention,
n=0
and
\Summands(M)=\varnothing
if
M=0
.)

Notation 2.5
:

Let
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
For simplicity, we sometimes use
\operatorname{smd}(M):=\{\!\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\!\}
instead of

\Summands(M)
, and identify
\operatorname{smd}(M)
with
\Summands(M)
by the
bijection
x\mapsto\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
.
The set of all bijections
\operatorname{smd}(M)\to\operatorname{smd}(N)
is denoted by

\Bij(\operatorname{smd}(M),\operatorname{smd}(N))
that is identified with the set

\Bij(\Summands(M),\Summands(N))
of bijections
\Summands(M)\to\Summands(N)

by regarding each
B\in\Bij(\operatorname{smd}(M),\operatorname{smd}(N))
as the bijection

\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}\mapsto B(\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}):=\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{B(x)}
for all
x\in\operatorname{smd}(M)
.

When we deal with morphisms by matrices, we need to fix an order of elements
of
\operatorname{smd}(M)
, thus in that case, we regard
\operatorname{smd}(M)
as a sequence

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

The follwoing is immediate from (
2.2
).

Lemma 2.6
:

Let
\alpha\colon M\to N
be a morphism in
\operatorname{prj}\mathbf{P}
, and assume that
the equalities

M=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{smd}(M)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
and
N=\bigoplus_{y\in\operatorname{smd}(N)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{y}

hold.
Then there exists a unique matrix

\operatorname{Mat}(\alpha):=[a_{x,y}]_{(x,y)\in\operatorname{smd}(M)\times\operatorname{smd}(N)}
over
\Bbbk
,
called the
matrix
of
\alpha
,
such that

\alpha=[a_{x,y}\,\rho(x\geq y)]_{(y,x)\in\operatorname{smd}(N)\times\operatorname{smd}(M)},

and that
a_{x,y}=0
unless
x\geq y
.

Remark 2.7
:

In the above, the matrix of
\alpha
is uniquely determined because of the condition
that
a_{x,y}=0
unless
x\geq y
.
Thanks to this condition, it holds that if
a_{x,y}\neq 0
, then
x\geq y
.

2.2
Monotone Maps and Kan Extensions

We now record how monotone maps between posets induce adjoint triples
between the corresponding module categories via Kan extensions. This
will be used repeatedly once we restrict to Galois connections.

A map
f:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
of posets is
monotone
if
x\leq y
in
\mathbf{Q}
implies
f(x)\leq f(y)
in
\mathbf{P}
. Viewing posets as thin categories, a functor
\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
is precisely a monotone map. Thus a monotone map
f:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
induces the restriction (precomposition) functor

f^{*}:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}},\qquad f^{*}N=N\circ f.

Proposition 2.8
:

For any monotone
f:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
, the restriction functor
f^{*}
is exact.
Moreover, the left and right Kan extensions along
f
exist; we denote them by

f_{!}:=\Lan_{f}
and
f_{*}:=\Ran_{f}
. By the defining property of Kan extensions
there are natural adjunctions
f_{!}\dashv f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
, so
f_{!}
is
right exact and
f_{*}
is left exact.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

\dashv

\dashv

2.3
Galois Connections

We recall that Galois connections are adjunctions between posets and underlie the identifications among
f_{!},f^{*},f_{*}
used later.

Definition 2.9
:

A
Galois connection
between posets
\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{P}
consists of monotone maps
f:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
and
g:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
such that

f(u)\leq x\iff u\leq g(x)\qquad(u\in\mathbf{Q},\ x\in\mathbf{P}).

Equivalently, viewing
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{P}
as thin categories, this means
f\dashv g
as functors. We write
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
, with
f
left adjoint and
g
right adjoint.

The following characterization of a Galois connection is corresponding to
that of an adjunction using a unit and a counit.
Since the verification is straightforward, we omit the proof.

Lemma 2.10
:

In the setting of Definition
2.9
,

f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
is a Galois connection if and only if

u\leq gf(u)\quad(u\in\mathbf{Q}),\quad\text{and}\quad fg(x)\leq x\quad(x\in\mathbf{P}).

If this is the case, we have
fgf=f
and
gfg=g
.

The existence of a natural transformation between monotone maps as functors is
verified by the following.
As it is easy to show, we omit the proof.

Lemma 2.11
:

Let
f,g\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
be monotone maps of posets.
Then there exists a natural transformation
f\Rightarrow g
if and only if

f(x)\leq g(x)
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
.
The latter condition is expressed by writing
f\leq g
.

To deal with pullbacks in the category of posets, we use the following.

Lemma 2.12
:

Consider a diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{S}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{R}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}

with a pullback square in the category of posets.
Then

(1)

If
\pi_{i}f=\pi_{i}g
for all
i=1,2
, then
f=g
.

(2)

If
\pi_{i}f\leq\pi_{i}g
for all
i=1,2
, then
f\leq g
.

Proof.

(1) This is immediate from the universality of the pullback.

(2) Recall that
\mathbf{R}
is constructed as the full subposet of
\mathbf{Q}_{1}\times\mathbf{Q}_{2}

with the underlying set given by

\mathbf{R}\ :=\ \bigl\{(q_{1},q_{2})\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}\times\mathbf{Q}_{2}:\sigma_{1}(q_{1})=\sigma_{2}(q_{2})\bigr\},

with projections
\pi_{1}:\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{Q}_{1}
and
\pi_{2}:\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{Q}_{2}
,
where the partial order on
\mathbf{Q}_{1}\times\mathbf{Q}_{2}
is defined as follows:
For any
(q_{1},q_{2}),(q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}\times\mathbf{Q}_{2}
,

(q_{1},q_{2})\leq(q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2})
if and only if
q_{i}\leq q^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,2
.
Then the assertion is clear from this definition.
∎

Corollary 2.13
:

If
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
is a Galois connection, then
g^{*}\dashv f^{*}
.

Corollary 2.14
:

In the same setting, there are natural isomorphisms
g^{*}\cong f_{!}
and
f^{*}\cong g_{*}
. In particular, the adjoint pairs

g^{*}\dashv f^{*},\qquad f_{!}\dashv f^{*},\qquad g^{*}\dashv g_{*}

are compatible via these isomorphisms.

Proof.

By uniqueness of adjoints in a
2
-category:
g^{*}
and
f_{!}
are both left adjoint to
f^{*}
, hence canonically isomorphic; dually,
f^{*}
and
g_{*}
are both right adjoint to
g^{*}
, hence canonically isomorphic.
∎

Corollary 2.15
:

If
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
is a Galois connection, then
g_{*}:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
is exact.

Proof.

Using Corollary
2.14
,
g_{*}\cong f^{*}
, and
f^{*}
is exact by Proposition
2.8
.
∎

Combining Corollary
2.15
with Lemma
2.1
yields:

Proposition 2.16
:

Let
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
be a Galois connection. If
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
is projective, then
g^{*}(M)
is projective in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.∎

The next basic facts will be used tacitly; they are immediate from the adjunction
f\dashv g
and we omit the proof.

Lemma 2.17
:

Let
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
be a Galois connection. Then:

(1)

The following are equivalent:
f
is surjective;
g
is injective;
f\circ g=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
.
If this is the case, this Galois connection is called a
Galois insertion
.
(For the proof, use Lemma
2.10
.)

(2)

For
x\in\mathbf{P}
,

g(x)=\max\{\,u\in\mathbf{Q}\mid f(u)\leq x\,\}.

In particular, if
f\circ g=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
, then

g(x)=\max\{\,u\in\mathbf{Q}\mid f(u)=x\,\}.

3
Galois Transport Distance

Fix a finite poset
\mathbf{P}
equipped with a metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}
. Motivated by optimal transport, we compare
\mathbf{P}
-modules by
transporting
them through a common “apex” poset
\mathbf{Q}
via
Galois insertions
.

Definition 3.1
:

Let
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
. A
Galois coupling

(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)

of the pair
(M,N)
consists of a finite poset
\mathbf{Q}
, two Galois insertions

f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g,\qquad h:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:i\quad\text{with}\quad f\circ g=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}=h\circ i,

and a module
\Gamma\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
such that
g^{*}\Gamma\cong M
and
i^{*}\Gamma\cong N
. Equivalently (Corollary
2.14
),
M\cong f_{!}\Gamma
and
N\cong h_{!}\Gamma
.

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

\dashv

\dashv

Definition 3.2
:

The
cost
of a coupling
(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,\ h\dashv i,\ \Gamma)
is

\cost(\Gamma):=\sup_{q\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\big(f(q),\,h(q)\big).

3.1
Composition of Couplings

We now show that Galois couplings compose.
Suppose we are given couplings

(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\ f_{1}\dashv g_{1},\ h_{1}\dashv i_{1},\ \Gamma_{1})\text{ of $(M,N)$ }\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\mathbf{Q}_{2},\ f_{2}\dashv g_{2},\ h_{2}\dashv i_{2},\ \Gamma_{2})\text{ of $(N,O)$},

displayed below:

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{R}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}

\dashv

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Our goal is to construct a module
\Psi\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
whose pullbacks
recover
M
and
O
.

Proposition 3.3
:

There exists
\Psi\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
and natural isomorphisms

(\iota_{1}g_{1})^{*}\Psi\cong M,\qquad(\iota_{2}i_{2})^{*}\Psi\cong O,

so that

\bigl(\mathbf{R},\ f_{1}\pi_{1}\dashv\iota_{1}g_{1},\ \ h_{2}\pi_{2}\dashv\iota_{2}i_{2},\ \ \Psi\bigr)

is a Galois coupling of
(M,O)
.
This composite is unique up to unique isomorphism.

Proof.

We first construct right adjoints of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.
Since
f_{2}g_{2}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
and
h_{1}i_{1}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{P}
, we have the following commutative diagrams
(without dashed arrows):

Hence by the universality of the pullback, there exists a unique monotone map
\iota_{1}

(resp.
\iota_{2}
) that makes the diagram on the left (resp. right) commutative.
Then we have
\pi_{1}\dashv\iota_{1}
.
Indeed, it is enough to show that
\pi_{1}\iota_{1}\leq 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
and
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{R}}\leq\iota_{1}\pi_{1}

by Lemma
2.10
.
The first inequality follows from the commutative diagram above.
To show the second one, it is enough to show that

\pi_{i}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{R}}\leq\pi_{i}\iota_{1}\pi_{1}
for all
i=1,2
by Lemma
2.12
(2).
For
i=1
, this follows from
\pi_{1}\iota_{1}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
.
For
i=2
, since
f_{2}\dashv g_{2}
, we have
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\leq g_{2}f_{2}
.
Therefore,
\pi_{2}\leq g_{2}f_{2}\pi_{2}=g_{2}h_{1}\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}\iota_{1}\pi_{1}
, as desired.
Similarly, we have
\pi_{2}\dashv\iota_{2}
.
Moreover we have
\pi_{i}\iota_{i}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2
by the commutative diagram above.
Thus these Galois connections are Galois insertions.

Next we construct
\Psi
.
Let
\eta_{1}\colon 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}\Rightarrow i_{1}h_{1}
(resp.
\eta_{2}\colon 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\Rightarrow g_{2}f_{2}
)
be the unit of the adjunction
h_{1}\dashv\iota_{1}
(resp.
f_{2}\dashv g_{2}
).
We set
\delta:=h_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=f_{2}\circ\pi_{2}\colon\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{P}
.
Then we have natural transformations

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{1}\circ\eta_{1}\circ\pi_{1}\colon\Gamma_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=\Gamma_{1}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{1}\circ i_{1}\circ h_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=\Gamma_{1}\circ i_{1}\circ\delta,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{2}\circ\eta_{2}\circ\pi_{2}\colon\Gamma_{2}\circ\pi_{2}=\Gamma_{2}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\circ\pi_{2}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ f_{2}\circ\pi_{2}=\Gamma_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ\delta.

These are regarded as morphisms in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
:

\sigma_{1}\colon\pi_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\to\delta^{*}i_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{2}\colon\pi_{2}^{*}(\Gamma_{2})\to\delta^{*}g_{2}^{*}(\Gamma_{2}).

Since
i_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\cong N\cong g_{2}^{*}(\Gamma_{2})
, there exists an isomorphism

\phi\colon i_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\to g_{2}^{*}(\Gamma_{2})
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
The
covariant
functor

\delta^{*}\colon\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
sends it to an isomorphism
\delta^{*}(\phi)\colon\delta^{*}i_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\to\delta^{*}g_{2}^{*}(\Gamma_{2})
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
.
Construct now the following pullback diagram in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{R}}
:

Then since

is an exact

functor by Proposition

,
it sends the diagram above to the pullback diagram

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.68384pt}{-25.35669pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{55.13232pt}{-1.79707pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here
\iota_{1}^{*}(\sigma_{2})
turns out to be an isomorphism.
Indeed, we have

\iota_{1}^{*}(\sigma_{2})=\sigma_{2}\circ\iota_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\circ\eta_{2}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\iota_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\circ\eta_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ h_{1}=\Gamma_{2}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}}.

The last equality follows from the fact that

\eta_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ h_{1}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}}
as natural transformations.
This can be verified as follows:
Since
\eta_{2}
is defined as the unique morphism

\eta_{2}(q_{2})\colon q_{2}\to g_{2}f_{2}(q_{2})
in
\mathbf{Q}_{2}

corresponding to the inequality

q_{2}\leq g_{2}f_{2}(q_{2})
for all
q_{2}\in\mathbf{Q}_{2}
,
we have
(\eta_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ h_{1})(q_{1})=\eta_{2}(g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1}))

is a morphism
g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})\to g_{2}f_{2}g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})

for all
q_{1}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}
, where
by Lemma
2.10
, we have

g_{2}f_{2}g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})=g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})
, and hence
\eta_{2}(g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1}))=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})}
.
Thus
\eta_{2}\circ g_{2}\circ h_{1}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}}
.
Since
\Gamma_{2}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}}=(\Gamma_{2}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})}))_{q_{1}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{2}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}(q_{1})})
are isomorphisms
for all
q_{1}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}
,
the natural transformation
\iota^{*}(\sigma_{2})=\Gamma_{2}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{g_{2}h_{1}}
is a natural isomorphism,
thus an isomorphism in the category
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}
.

Since the pullback of isomorphisms are isomorphisms in abelian
categories,

\iota^{*}(\theta_{1})
is also an isomorphism.
Hence we have
\iota_{1}^{*}(\Psi)\cong\iota_{1}^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})=(\pi_{1}\iota_{1})^{*}(\Gamma_{1})=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})=\Gamma_{1}
.
Similarly, we have
\iota_{2}^{*}(\Psi)\cong\Gamma_{2}
.
As a consequence, we have

(\iota_{1}g_{1})^{*}(\Psi)=g_{1}^{*}\iota_{1}^{*}(\Psi)\cong g_{1}^{*}(\Gamma_{1})\cong M,\quad\text{and similarly}\quad(\iota_{2}i_{2})^{*}\Psi\cong O.\qed

3.2
Transport Distance

With composition available, we now define the transport distance and record its basic properties.

Definition 3.4
:

The
Galois transport distance
between
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
is

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N):=\inf\{\ \cost(\Gamma)\ \mid\ \Gamma\ \text{is a Galois coupling of }(M,N)\ \}.

If there is no Galois coupling between
M
and
N
, set
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=\infty
.

Lemma 3.5
:

If
\Gamma_{1}
is a coupling for
(M,N)
and
\Gamma_{2}
is a coupling for
(N,O)
, and
\Psi
is their composite from Proposition
3.3
, then

\cost(\Psi)\ \leq\ \cost(\Gamma_{1})\ +\ \cost(\Gamma_{2}).

Proof.

For
r\in\mathbf{R}
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathbf{P}}\big((f_{1}\pi_{1})(r),\ (h_{2}\pi_{2})(r)\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathbf{P}}\big((f_{1}\pi_{1})(r),\ (h_{1}\pi_{1})(r)\big)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{d_{\mathbf{P}}\big((h_{1}\pi_{1})(r),\ (f_{2}\pi_{2})(r)\big)}_{=\,0}

\displaystyle+d_{\mathbf{P}}\big((f_{2}\pi_{2})(r),\ (h_{2}\pi_{2})(r)\big),

since
\delta=h_{1}\pi_{1}=f_{2}\pi_{2}
. Taking suprema and observing

\sup_{r\in\mathbf{R}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\big(f_{1}\pi_{1}(r),h_{1}\pi_{1}(r)\big)=\sup_{q_{1}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\big(f_{1}(q_{1}),h_{1}(q_{1})\big)=\cost(\Gamma_{1}),

(and similarly for
\Gamma_{2}
) yields the claim.
∎

Proposition 3.6
:

For a finite poset
\mathbf{P}
with metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}
, the function

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}:\ \mathrm{Ob}(\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}})\times\mathrm{Ob}(\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}})\longrightarrow[0,+\infty]

is an extended pseudometric.

Proof.

Nonnegativity is immediate. For any
M
, the identity coupling
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{P}
,
f=h=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
,
g=i=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
,
\Gamma=M
has cost
0
, so
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,M)=0
. Symmetry holds because swapping the two insertion legs
(f\dashv g,h\dashv i)
of any coupling gives a coupling for
(N,M)
with the same cost (the metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}
is symmetric). The triangle inequality follows from Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
:

On isomorphism classes,
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}
is an extended metric: if
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=0
then
M\cong N
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{P}
is finite, the set
\{d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,y)\mid x,y\in\mathbf{P}\}
is finite; hence every coupling has cost in this finite set, and the infimum in the definition of
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)
is a
minimum
. If
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=0
, there exists a coupling with
\cost(\Gamma)=0
, so
d_{\mathbf{P}}(f(q),h(q))=0
for all
q\in\mathbf{Q}
, hence
f(q)=h(q)
and therefore
f=h
as maps
\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
. Using Corollary
2.14
,

M\ \cong\ f_{!}\Gamma\ =\ h_{!}\Gamma\ \cong\ N,

so
M\cong N
.
∎

Relation to interleavings.

Over the totally ordered real line, the Galois transport distance coincides
with the classical interleaving distance. This equivalence was first observed
by Gülen and McCleary
[
22
]
; for the convenience of the
reader, we include a self-contained proof in Appendix
A
.

3.3
Examples

We now present two illustrative examples—one in the 1-parameter setting
and one in the 2-parameter setting.
These will serve as running test cases throughout the paper for the
Galois transport distance and its comparison with later constructions.
First we collect terminologies for later use.

Recall that the
Hasse quiver

H(\mathbf{P})
of a finite poset
\mathbf{P}

is defined as follows:
The vertex set of
H(\mathbf{P})
is given by
\mathbf{P}
itself.
For any
a,b\in\mathbf{P}
, the number of arrows from
a
to
b

in
H(\mathbf{P})
is at most one, and it is one if and only if

a<b
and there exist no
c\in\mathbf{P}

such that
a<c<b
.
We sometimes express finite posets as their Hasse quivers below.
Note that any
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
is expressed as a representation
of the quiver
H(\mathbf{P})
satisfying the full commutativity relations.

Definition 3.8
:

Let
\mathbf{P}
be a finite poset.

(1) A full subposet
I
of
\mathbf{P}
is said to be
connected
(resp.
convex in
\mathbf{P}
) if
H(I)
is connected as a graph (resp. if
a\leq c\leq b
in
\mathbf{P}
with
a,b\in I
implies
c\in I
).
Then
I
is called a
generalized interval

(or simply
interval
) in
\mathbf{P}
if
it is both connected and convex in
\mathbf{P}
.
By
\operatorname{gInt}\mathbf{P}
we denote the set of all generalized intervals.

(2) A subset
A
of
\mathbf{P}
is called an
antichain
in
\mathbf{P}
if any distinct elements of
A
are incomparable. We denote by
\operatorname{Ac}(\mathbf{P})
the set of all antichains in
\mathbf{P}
.
According to
[
7
]
,
for any
A,B\in\operatorname{Ac}(\mathbf{P})
, we define
A\leq B
if for any
a\in A
, there exists
b_{a}\in B
such that
a\leq b_{a}
, and for any
b\in B
, there exists
a_{b}\in A
such that
a_{b}\leq b

1
1

1

In other words,
A\leq B
if and only if
“
A\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}B
and
B\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}A
”
if and only if
“
\mathord{\downarrow}A\subseteq\mathord{\downarrow}B
and
\mathord{\uparrow}B\subseteq\mathord{\uparrow}A
”,
where
\mathord{\uparrow}A:=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid a\leq x\text{ for some }a\in A\}
, the up-set of
A
and
\mathord{\downarrow}B:=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid x\leq b\text{ for some }b\in B\}
, the down-set of
B
.
.
In this case, we define

[A,B]:=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid a\leq x\leq b\text{ for some }a\in A\text{ and for some }b\in B\}.

It is known that

\{[A,B]\mid A,B\in\operatorname{Ac}(\mathbf{P}),\ A\leq B\}

forms the set of all convex subsets in
\mathbf{P}

(e.g., see
[
7
, Propossition 2.2]
,
[
4
, Lemma 3.10]
).
Hence any generalized interval
I
has the form
I=[A,B]
, where

A
(resp.
B
) is the set of minimal (resp. maximal) elements in
I

(here
A,B\in\operatorname{Ac}(\mathbf{P})
automatically).

Note here that a full subposet
S
of
\mathbf{P}
is an interval
in the usual sense, namely,
S=[a,b]
for some
a\leq b
in
\mathbf{P}

if and only if
S
is a generalized interval
[A,B]
such that
both
A
and
B
are singletons.

(3) For any generalized interval
I
in
\mathbf{P}
, a
\mathbf{P}
-module
V_{I}

(called the
interval module for
I
) is defined by
V_{I}(x):=\Bbbk
if
x\in I
,
V_{I}(x):=0
otherwise; and

V_{I}(\alpha):=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk}
if
\alpha
is an arrow
a\to b
with
a,b\in I
,
V_{I}(\alpha):=0
otherwise.
As is easily seen, the endomorphism algebra of
V_{I}
is isomorphic
to
\Bbbk
, and hence it is an indecomopsable
\mathbf{P}
-module.
To emphasize the fact that
V_{I}
is a
\mathbf{P}
-module, we denote it
by
V^{\mathbf{P}}_{I}
.

Example 3.9
:

Let
\mathbf{P}=\{1<2<3<4\}
with metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}(i,j)=|i-j|
.
Define

M:=V_{[1,1]}\oplus V_{[2,3]},\qquad N:=V_{[2,3]}.

To construct a low-cost coupling, take the apex poset

\mathbf{Q}:=\{0<1<2<3<4<5\}
and define
f,h:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}

and
g,i\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
by the following tables:

\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}q&0&1&2&3&4&5\\
\hline\cr f(q)&1&1&2&3&4&4\\
h(q)&1&2&2&3&4&4\\
\hline\cr d_{\mathbf{P}}(f(q),h(q))&0&1&0&0&0&0\end{array},\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}p&1&2&3&4\\
\hline\cr g(p)&1&2&3&5\\
i(p)&0&2&3&5\end{array}.

A short computation shows that
f\dashv g
and
h\dashv i
are Galois insertions.
Let

\Gamma:=V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{[1,1]}\ \oplus\ V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{[2,3]}.

Then

g^{*}\Gamma\cong M,\qquad i^{*}\Gamma\cong N,

and therefore
(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)
is a Galois coupling
of the pair
(M,N)
.
The coupling cost is

\cost(\Gamma)=\sup_{q\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}(f(q),h(q))=1,

and hence
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)\leq 1
.
To see that this bound is sharp, note that
\mathbf{P}
is finite, so

d_{\mathbf{P}}
takes only integer values. A coupling of cost
<1
would
therefore have cost
0
, which would force
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=0
. By
Corollary
3.7
, this would imply
M\cong N
, which
is false. Hence no cost-
0
coupling exists.
Thus
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)\geq 1
.
Combining the upper and lower bounds, we conclude

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=1.

Before giving the next examples, we first give a general way of constructing Galois couplings.
Similar constructions are found in
[
24
, Lemma 6.5]
.
For a
\mathbf{P}
-module
M
, we set
\operatorname{supp}M:=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid M(x)\neq 0\}
, the support of
M
, and
for a monotone map
\sigma\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{P}
, we set
\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}
to be the set

\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid\sigma(x)=x\}
of fixed points of
\mathbf{P}
by
\sigma
.
Recall that a monotone map
\sigma
above is called a
translation
if

x\leq\sigma(x)
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
.

Proposition 3.10
:

Let
\mathbf{P}
be a finite poset with metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}
,
and
\sigma\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{P}
a translation.
Then we can construct a finite poset
\mathbf{Q}
and Galois insertions

f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
and

h:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:i
as follows.

(1) Take the disjoint union

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\;:=\;\mathbf{P}_{L}\sqcup\mathbf{P}_{R}

of two copies of
\mathbf{P}
, more explicitly,

\mathbf{P}_{L}:=\{\,x_{L}:=(x,0)\mid x\in\mathbf{P}\,\},\ \mathbf{P}_{R}:=\{\,x_{R}:=(x,1)\mid x\in\mathbf{P}\,\},\ \text{and }\ \hat{\mathbf{Q}}:=\mathbf{P}_{L}\cup\mathbf{P}_{R}.

For each
S\subseteq\mathbf{P}
, we set
S_{T}:=\{x_{T}\mid x\in S\}
for all
T\in\{L,R\}
.

(2) We define a binary relation
\leq
on
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
as follows.
For any
x,y\in\mathbf{P}
, and
i,j\in\{0,1\}
,

(x,i)\leq(y,j)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\begin{cases}\sigma(x)\leq y&\text{if $i\neq j$},\\
x\leq y&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3) Then this is a preorder on
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
.
Denote by
\sim_{\sigma}
the equivalence relation with respect to this preorder, namely,
for any
x\,y\in\mathbf{P}
, and
i,j\in\{0,1\}
,

(x,i)\sim_{\sigma}(y,j)
if and only if
(x,i)\leq(y,j)
and
(y,j)\leq(x,i)
.

(4) We define
\mathbf{Q}
to be the quotient poset
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}/\!\!\sim_{\sigma}
, and denote
by
[\operatorname{-}]\colon\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\to\mathbf{Q},\,u\mapsto[u]
the canonical surjection.
We set
[S]:=\{[s]\mid s\in S\}
for all
S\subseteq\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
.
For each
T\in\{L,R\}
,
this restricts to a bijection
\mathbf{P}_{T}\to[\mathbf{P}_{T}]
,
by which we identify
\mathbf{P}_{T}
and
[\mathbf{P}_{T}]
.

(5) For each
(x,i)\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have

[(x,i)]=\begin{cases}\{x_{L},x_{R}\}&\text{if }\sigma(x)=x,\\
\{(x,i)\}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus
\mathbf{Q}
is obtained from
\mathbf{P}_{L}
and
\mathbf{P}_{R}
by making the identifications

x_{L}=x_{R}
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}
:

\mathbf{P}_{L}\cup\mathbf{P}_{R}=\mathbf{Q},\ \text{and }\ \mathbf{P}_{L}\cap\mathbf{P}_{R}=[\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}_{L}]=[\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}_{R}].

(3.1)

(6) The following defines a Galois insertion
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
.

g(x):=x_{L},\quad\text{and}\quad f(u):=\begin{cases}x&\text{if $u=x_{L}$ for some $x\in\mathbf{P}$},\\
\sigma(x)&\text{if $u=x_{R}$ for some $x\in\mathbf{P}$}.\end{cases}

Note that
f
is well-defined by (
3.1
).

(7) Similarly, the following defines a Galois insertion
h:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:i
.

i(x):=x_{R},\quad\text{and}\quad h(u):=\begin{cases}\sigma(x)&\text{if $u=x_{L}$ for some $x\in\mathbf{P}$},\\
x&\text{if $u=x_{R}$ for some $x\in\mathbf{P}$}.\end{cases}

(8)
Now let
M
and
N
be
\mathbf{P}
-modules.
If there exists a
\mathbf{Q}
-module
\Gamma
such that

g^{*}(\Gamma)\cong M
and
i^{*}(\Gamma)\cong N
, then

(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)
is a Galois coupling
for
(M,N)
by definition.
If this is the case, the
\cost(\Gamma)
is given by

\cost(\Gamma)=\sup_{u\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}(f(u),h(u))=\sup_{x\in\mathbf{P}}d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,\sigma(x)).

For example, If

M=N\sigma,

(3.2)

then we can construct a Galois coupling

(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)

by setting
\Gamma:=h^{*}(N)
.

Proof.

(3) For any
x\in\mathbf{P}
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, since
x\leq x
, we have
(x,i)\leq(x,i)
.

Let
(x,i),(y,j),(z,k)\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
, and assume that

(x,i)\leq(y,j)
and
(y,j)\leq(z,k)
.
Then we have four cases: (a)
i=j
,
j=k
; (b)
i=j
,
j\neq k
;
(c)
i\neq j
,
j=k
; and (d)
i\neq j
,
j\neq k
.
It is easy to see that in any case, we have
(x,i)\leq(z,k)
by definition.

(5)
Let
(x,i),(y,j)\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
, and assume that

(x,i)\sim_{\sigma}(y,j)
. Then

Case
i=j
: In this case, we have
x\leq y
and
y\leq x
, and hence
x=y
.

Case
i\neq j
: In this case, we have
\sigma(x)\leq y
and
\sigma(y)\leq x
, and hence

\sigma^{2}(x)\leq\sigma(y)\leq x\leq\sigma(x)\leq\sigma^{2}(x)
, which shows that

\sigma(x)=x=\sigma(y)
. By symmetry we have
\sigma(y)=y
.
Thus
x=y
and
\sigma(x)=x
.
This shows statement (5).

(6) First we show that for any
u\in\mathbf{Q}
, we have
f(u)=\min\{y\in\mathbf{P}\mid u\leq g(y)\}
.
When
u=[x_{L}]
for some
x\in\mathbf{P}
,
we have the following equivalences for any
y\in\mathbf{P}
:

u\leq g(y)\Longleftrightarrow[x_{L}]\leq[y_{L}]\Longleftrightarrow x\leq y.

Hence
\min\{y\in\mathbf{P}\mid u\leq g(y)\}=x=f(u)
.

When
u=[x_{R}]
for some
x\in\mathbf{P}
, we have the follwoing equivalences for any
y\in\mathbf{P}
:

u\leq g(y)\Longleftrightarrow[x_{R}]\leq[y_{L}]\Longleftrightarrow\sigma(x)\leq y.

Hence
\min\{y\in\mathbf{P}\mid u\leq g(y)\}=\sigma(x)=f(u)
.

Second, we show that for any
x\in\mathbf{P}
, we have
g(x)=\max\{u\in\mathbf{Q}\mid f(u)\leq x\}
.
Note that

\{u\in\mathbf{Q}\mid f(u)\leq x\}=\{[y_{L}]\mid y\in\mathbf{P},y\leq x\}\cup\{[z_{R}]\mid z\in\mathbf{P},\sigma(z)\leq x\}.

Then clearly
[x_{L}]=\max\{[y_{L}]\mid y\in\mathbf{P},y\leq x\}
, and for any
z\in\mathbf{P}
,
if
\sigma(z)\leq x
, then
[z_{R}]\leq[x_{L}]
, which shows that

\max\{u\in\mathbf{Q}\mid f(u)\leq x\}=[x_{L}]=g(x)
.

(8) This is verified as follows:

g^{*}(\Gamma)=N\circ h\circ g=N\circ\sigma=M,\ \text{and }\ i^{*}(\Gamma)=N\circ h\circ i=N\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}=N.

∎

We now give further sufficient conditions when
\Gamma
can be constructed from
M,N
in the setting above.

Proposition 3.11
:

Consider the same setting as in Proposition
3.10
.
We define a subposet
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{Q}
as follows:
The underlying set of
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
is the same as that of
\mathbf{Q}
.
The partial order
\leq^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
is defined by

[(x,i)]\leq^{\prime}[(y,j)]:\Longleftrightarrow i=j\text{ and }x\leq y\text{ in }\mathbf{P}

for all
(x,i),(y,j)\in\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
.
For each
T\in\{L,R\}
, denote by
p_{T}\colon\mathbf{P}_{T}\to\mathbf{P}
,

x_{T}\mapsto x
the first projection.

(1) Assume that

M|_{\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}}=N|_{\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}}

(3.3)

(for example, this trivially holds if

\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}\cap\operatorname{supp}M=\varnothing=\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}\cap\operatorname{supp}N
),
Then we define
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}
by

\Gamma^{\prime}|_{\mathbf{P}_{L}}=M\circ p_{L},\qquad\Gamma^{\prime}|_{\mathbf{P}_{R}}=N\circ p_{R},

which is well-defined by (
3.3
) and (
3.1
).
Regard
\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]
as
\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]
-
\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}]
-bimodule via
the inclusion
q\colon\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{Q}
, and consider the
adjoint pair given by this bimodule:

\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]\otimes_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}]}\operatorname{-}\colon\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}],\operatorname{-}).

Set
\Gamma
to be the the left Kan extension of
\Gamma^{\prime}
along
q
:

\Gamma:=\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]\otimes_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}]}\Gamma^{\prime}.

In addition, assume that

\Gamma|_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}\cong\Gamma^{\prime}

(this holds for example, if the unit morphism

\eta_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}],\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]\otimes_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}]}\Gamma^{\prime})

is an isomorphism). Then

(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)
is a Galois coupling for
(M,N)
.

(2) For each
1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Let
M=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}V_{I_{i}}^{\mathbf{P}}
and

N=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}V_{J_{j}}^{\mathbf{P}}
for some
I_{i},J_{j}\in\operatorname{gInt}\mathbf{P}

with
i\in[m],j\in[n]
for some
1\leq m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then it is obvious that
I_{i,L}:=(I_{i})_{L}
and
J_{j,R}:=(J_{j})_{R}
are in
\operatorname{gInt}\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
for all
i\in[m],j\in[n]
.
Assume that
I_{i,L}
and
J_{j,R}
are convex also in
\mathbf{Q}
, or equivalently,

I_{i,L},J_{j,R}\in\operatorname{gInt}\mathbf{Q}
for all
i\in[m],j\in[n]
.
Then by setting

\Gamma:=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}V_{I_{i,L}}^{\mathbf{Q}}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}V_{J_{j,R}}^{\mathbf{Q}}\right)

We have a Galois coupling
(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)

for
(M,N)
.

Proof.

We can define a monotone map
g^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}
by setting

g^{\prime}(x):=g(x)
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
. Then
g=q\circ g^{\prime}
as monotone maps.

(1) By definition of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and the additional assumption,
we have the following commutative diagrams:

\Rightarrow
” is commutative
up to natural isomorphsim.
Therefore,
g^{*}(\Gamma)=\Gamma\circ g\cong M
.
Similarly, we have
i^{*}(\Gamma)\cong N
.

(2)
Set
{\mathcal{I}}:=\{I_{i,L},J_{j,R}\mid i\in[m],n\in[n]\}
.
Since
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\operatorname{gInt}\mathbf{Q}
, we have

V_{I}^{\mathbf{Q}}\circ q^{\prime}=V_{I}^{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}
for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}
.
By definition of interval modules, we have the following commutative diagram for all
i\in[m]
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Therefore,
g^{*}(V_{I_{i,L}}^{\mathbf{Q}})=V_{I_{i,L}}^{\mathbf{Q}}\circ g=V_{I_{i}}^{\mathbf{P}}
for all
i\in[m]
.
Similarly, we have
i^{*}(V_{I_{j,R}}^{\mathbf{Q}})=V_{I_{j}}^{\mathbf{P}}

for all
j\in[n]
.
Thus we have
g^{*}(\Gamma)=M
and
i^{*}(\Gamma)=N
.
∎

Example 3.12
:

Let
\mathbf{P}:=\{1,2,3\}^{2}
with the product order and

d_{\mathbf{P}}((i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\max\{|i-i^{\prime}|,|j-j^{\prime}|\}
.
We denote
(x,y)
simply by
xy
for all
x,y\in\{1,2,3\}
.

(3.4)

We define a monotone map
\sigma\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{P}
by

\sigma(xy):=(\min\{x+1,3\},\min\{y+1,3\})
for each

xy\in\mathbf{P}
.
Then as is easily seen,
\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}=\{33\}
.
To couple the modules
M,N
, we apply Proposition
3.10
with this
\sigma
.
In this case,
it is easy to verify that
[12,12]_{L},[21,31]_{L},[22,\{23,32\}]_{R}
are
convex in
\mathbf{Q}
.
Hence by Proposition
3.11
(2),

\Gamma:=V_{[12,12]_{L}}^{\mathbf{Q}}\oplus V_{[21,31]_{L}}^{\mathbf{Q}}\oplus V_{[22,\{23,32\}]_{R}}^{\mathbf{Q}}

defines
a Galois coupling
(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)
.
Then by Proposition
3.10
,
it follows from the definition of
\sigma
that

\cost(\Gamma)\;=\;\sup_{x\in\mathbf{P}}d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,\sigma(x))=1,

and therefore

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)\;\leq\;1.

To see that this bound is sharp, note that
d_{\mathbf{P}}
takes only integer values.
Thus a cost strictly smaller than
1
would have to be
0
.
A cost-
0
coupling forces
f=h
and hence
M\cong N

by Corollary
3.7
, but the modules are not isomorphic.
Combining the upper and lower bounds yields

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)=1.

Remark 3.13
:

In Example
3.12
, it is also possible to apply Proposition

3.12
(1) instead of (2).
In that case, we have a different
\Gamma
(to distinguish, we denote it
by
\Gamma^{!}
).
