\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where for each additional arrow
\pi
, we have

\Gamma^{!}(\pi)=\begin{cases}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk}&\text{if $\pi=\pi_{\sigma(x)_{R},x_{L}}$ for $x=21,31$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

whereas
\Gamma(\pi)=0
for all additional arrows
\pi
.
We remark that the Galois coupling given by

(\mathbf{Q},\ f\dashv g,\ h\dashv i,\ \Gamma^{!})

is precisely a
1
-interleaving in the sense of Lesnick’s theory of multiparameter persistence
[
28
]
.

The following example will be used to illustrate the proof of
Theorem
5.2
.

Example 3.14
:

Let
\mathbf{P}
be a poset with
H(\mathbf{P})=(1\to 2)
with metric
d_{\mathbf{P}}(a,b)=|a-b|

for all
a,b\in\mathbf{P}
, and
define a monotone map
\sigma\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{P}
by
\sigma(1)=2=\sigma(2)
.
Then
\mathbf{P}^{\sigma}=\{2\}
.
Apply Proposition
3.10
to construct
a finite poset
\mathbf{Q}
and Galois insertions

f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
and

h:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:i
.
In this case,
\mathbf{Q}
is expressed by its Hasse quiver

H(\mathbf{Q})=(1_{L}\to 2\leftarrow 1_{R}),

where we set
2:=2_{L}=2_{R}
.

f,h\colon\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
are defined as follows:
for any
x\in\mathbf{P}
,

f(x_{L}):=x,\,f(1_{R}):=2,\quad\text{and}\quad h(x_{R}):=x,\,h(1_{L}):=2.

Their right adjoints
g,i\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
are
defined as follows, respectively: for any
x\in\mathbf{P}
,

g(x):=x_{L},\quad\text{and}\quad i(x):=x_{R}.

Consider
\mathbf{P}
-modules
M=V_{\mathbf{P}}\oplus V_{\{1\}}
and

N=V_{\mathbf{P}}
.
They are visualized as representations of the quiver
H(\mathbf{P})
as follows:

M=\Bbbk^{2}\xrightarrow{[1\ 0]}\Bbbk,\quad N=\Bbbk\xrightarrow{1}\Bbbk.

It is obvious that (
3.3
) is satisfied, and
note that
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}=\mathbf{Q}
in this case.
Hence by Proposition
3.11
(1),
we can take
\Gamma:=\Gamma^{\prime}
, which is givne by

(\Bbbk^{2}\xrightarrow{[1\ 0]}\Bbbk\xleftarrow{1}\Bbbk)
as a
representation of
H(\mathbf{Q})
, and this defines a Galois coupling

(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,h\dashv i,\Gamma)
for
(M,N)
.

Since
d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,\sigma(x))=1,0

for
x=1,2
, respectively, we have

\cost(\Gamma)=1
.
Hence
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)\leq 1
.
Moreover, since
M\not\cong N
, we have
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)>0

by Corollary
3.7
.
Therefore,
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)=1
because the image of

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}
consists of integers in this case.

4
Bottleneck Distance

In this section we define a second distance, expressed entirely in terms
of minimal projective resolutions, which we will compare to the Galois
transport distance in Section
3
.
Throughout this section,
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
is a finite metric poset.

Let
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then by
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
we denote a minimal projective resolution of
M
,
which is unique up to isomorphism of complexes, and
by
\mathsf{Res}(M)
the set of all projective resolutions of
M
.

Let
R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=(R_{i},\partial^{R}_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathsf{Res}(M)
.
Then by Lemmas
2.4
and
2.6
,
we may set

R_{i}=\bigoplus_{x^{(i)}\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x^{(i)}}

(4.1)

for all
i\geq 0
, and

\partial^{R}_{i}=[a_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}^{(i)}\,\rho(x^{(i+1)}\geq x^{(i)})]_{(x^{i+1},x^{i})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\colon R_{i+1}\to R_{i}

(4.2)

with

[a_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}^{(i)}]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}=\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{R}_{i})
for all
i\geq 0
.

The
size vector

|R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
of
R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
is
defined as
|R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|:=(|R_{i}|)_{i\geq 0}
. For indecomposable projectives
U,V
(identified with representables
U\cong\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
,
V\cong\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{y}
), we set

\dist(U,V):=d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,y).

If
E
is any projective object in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, the mapping cone
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})
is the two-term contractible complex

\cdots\to 0\to\overset{\begin{smallmatrix}(\deg\,1)\\
\ \end{smallmatrix}}{E}\xrightarrow{\ 1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E}\ }\overset{\begin{smallmatrix}(\deg\,0)\\
\ \end{smallmatrix}}{E}\to 0\to\cdots,

concentrated in degrees 0 and 1; its shift
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[a]
places the two copies of
E
in degrees
a
and
a+1
. Direct-summing
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[a]
with a projective resolution leaves the resolved module unchanged and yields a chain-homotopy equivalent resolution (we call this
padding by a contractible cone
).

Note that for any finite poset
\mathbf{P}
,
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]
has finite global dimension.
Hence as is well-known, the following holds.

Lemma 4.1
:

Every projective resolution of
M
is obtained from the minimal one by padding with contractible cones:

\mathsf{Res}(M)\ =\ \Bigl\{\ P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}\ \oplus\ \bigoplus_{i\in[n]}\,\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E_{i}})[a_{i}]\ \Bigm|\ n\in\mathbb{N},\ a_{i}\in\mathbb{N},\ E_{i}\in\operatorname{prj}\mathbf{P}\ \Bigr\}.

Remark 4.2
:

For
b\in\mathbf{P}
, let
1_{b}
denote the interval
\mathbf{P}
-module

V_{\{b\}}
of the singleton

\{b\}
(a spread in the sense of
[
21
]
), and let
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.

In the language of
[
21
]
, the Möbius cohomology of
M
at
b

is computed by the Ext–groups

\Ext^{d}_{\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}(1_{b},M),

and the Möbius homology groups
H^{\downarrow}_{d}M(b)
of
[
33
]

are, for
\mathsf{vec}
, canonically dual:

H^{\downarrow}_{d}M(b)\ \cong\ \Ext^{d}_{\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}(1_{b},M)^{\vee},

where
(-)^{\vee}
denotes
\Bbbk
-linear dual.
In particular, if
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}\to M
is a minimal projective resolution, then the
Ext–groups (and hence the Möbius homology at
b
) are functorially determined
by
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
via the standard computation

\Ext^{d}_{\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}(1_{b},M)\ \cong\ H^{d}\!\bigl(\Hom_{\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{1_{b}},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M})\bigr),

for any projective resolution
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{1_{b}}\to 1_{b}
.
Thus, up to duality, the Möbius homology of
M
is encoded in its minimal
projective resolution.

We will not use this identification in the sequel, but it provides a conceptual
bridge between the Möbius homology of
[
33
]
and the
projective–resolution viewpoint developed in this paper; full details in the
(cohomological) setting can be found in
[
21
]
.

4.1
Matchings

We now define degreewise matchings between two projective resolutions. Given
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, consider pairs of projective resolutions with the same size vector:

\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\ :=\ \bigl\{\,(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(M)\times\mathsf{Res}(N)\ \bigm|\ |E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|\,\bigr\}.

Proposition 4.3
:

Assume that
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
and
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}
are bounded complexes.
Then they have the same alternating sum

\sum_{i\geq 0}(-1)^{i}|P^{M}_{i}|\ =\ \sum_{i\geq 0}(-1)^{i}|P^{N}_{i}|

if and only if
\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

Let
p=|P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
,
q=|P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
,
e_{i}:=(\delta_{j,i})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\mathbb{N})}
, and
e^{(i)}:=e_{i}+e_{i+1}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
|\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}})[i]|=e^{(i)}
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
We set
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}^{(\mathbb{N})}\to\mathbb{Z}
to be
the alternating-sum map:
\alpha(r)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}r_{i}
(
r\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\mathbb{N})}
).
Then as is easily seen, the kernel of
\alpha
is generated by the set

\{e^{(i)}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
as an abelian group:

\operatorname{Ker}\alpha=\langle e^{(i)}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
(The proof is similar to and easier than
that of Lemma
4.9
, and we omit it).

(
\Rightarrow
).
Assume
\alpha(p)=\alpha(q)
.
Then we have
p-q\in\operatorname{Ker}\alpha=\langle e^{(i)}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
, and hence

p-q=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}k_{i}e^{(i)}=\sum_{i\in S_{+}}k_{i}e^{(i)}-\sum_{i\in S_{-}}|k_{i}|e^{(i)},\quad\text{and thus}\quad p+\sum_{i\in S_{-}}|k_{i}|e^{(i)}=q+\sum_{i\in S_{+}}k_{i}e^{(i)}

for some
(k_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\mathbb{N})}
, where
S_{+}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid k_{i}\geq 0\},\,S_{-}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid k_{i}<0\}
.
Take any
x\in\mathbf{P}
, and set
E:=\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}
.
Then the last equality above shows that

\left|P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i\in S_{-}}\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[i]\right)^{(k_{i})}\right|=\left|P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i\in S_{+}}\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[i]\right)^{(k_{i})}\right|.

(
\Leftarrow
).
The argument above can be reversed.
∎

Definition 4.4
:

Let
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})
, and set

\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{E}_{i})=[a_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}^{(i)}]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(E_{i})},\quad\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{i})=[b_{y^{(i+1)},y^{(i)}}^{(i)}]_{(y^{(i+1)},y^{()})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(F_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(F_{i})}

for all
i\geq 0
.
Then a
pre-matching
of
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
is a family of degreewise bijections
(see Notation
2.5
)

B=(B_{i})_{i\geq 0},\quad B_{i}\in\Bij(\operatorname{smd}(E_{i}),\,\operatorname{smd}(F_{i})).

A
matching
of
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
is a pre-matching of
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

satisfying the
compatibility condition on differentials
:

a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}=b^{(i)}_{B_{i+1}(x^{(i+1)}),B_{i}(x^{(i)})}

for all
i\geq 0
,
x^{(i)}\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i}),x^{(i+1)}\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i+1})
.
Note that this condition implies that if
a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}\neq 0
, then
B_{i+1}(x^{(i+1)})\geq B_{i}(x^{(i)})
.

By
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
(resp.
\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
),
we denote the set of matchings (resp. pre-matchings) of
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.

Define the
cost
of a pre-matching
B
as the
L^{\infty}
-type aggregate of the underlying poset metric,

\cost(B)\ :=\ \sup\bigl\{\,\dist\bigl(U,\ B_{i}(U)\bigr)\ \bigm|\ i\geq 0,\ U\in\Summands(E_{i})\,\bigr\}=\sup\{d_{\mathbf{P}}(x,B_{i}(x))\mid i\geq 0,x\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i})\}.

(Equivalently,
\cost(B)=\sup\{\dist(B_{i}^{-1}(V),V)\mid i\geq 0,\ V\in\Summands(F_{i})\}
.)
We refer to the quantity

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ :=\ \inf_{\,B\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})}\ \cost(B)

as the
matching distance
for resolutions with a fixed size vector.
It is defined to be infinity if
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=\varnothing
.

Lemma 4.5
:

Let
M,N,O\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, and

(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,

(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{O}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Then

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ +\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).

Proof.

If
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
or
(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
has no matching, then
the right hand side is infinity, and the inequality holds.
Therefore, we may assume that both of them have some matchings.
Choose matchings

B\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}),\qquad C\in\Match(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).

(4.3)

Define
D\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
degreewise by

D_{i}:=C_{i}\circ B_{i}\qquad(i\geq 0),

so that
D_{i}\colon\Summands(E_{i})\to\Summands(G_{i})
is again a bijection.
Then
D\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Moreover,
D
satisfies the compatibility condition on differentials for
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,
and hence
D\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Indeed, set
\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{E}_{i}),\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{i})
to be as in Definition

4.4
, and

\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{G}_{i}):=[c_{z^{(i+1)},z^{(i)}}^{(i)}]_{(z^{(i+1)},z^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(G_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(G_{i})}

for all
i\geq 0
.
Then it follows from (
4.3
) that

a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}=b^{(i)}_{B_{i+1}(x^{(i+1)}),B_{i}(x^{(i)})}=c^{(i)}_{C_{i+1}(B_{i+1}(x^{(i+1)})),C_{i}(B_{i}(x^{(i)}))}=c^{(i)}_{D_{i+1}(x^{(i+1)}),D_{i}(x^{(i)})}

for all
i\geq 0
,
x^{(i)}\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i}),x^{(i+1)}\in\operatorname{smd}(E_{i+1})
.

For any
i
and any
U\in\Summands(E_{i})
, the metric
\dist
on indecomposable
projectives satisfies

\dist\bigl(U,D_{i}(U)\bigr)=\dist\bigl(U,C_{i}(B_{i}(U))\bigr)\leq\dist\bigl(U,B_{i}(U)\bigr)+\dist\bigl(B_{i}(U),C_{i}(B_{i}(U))\bigr).

Taking the supremum over all
i
and
U
gives

\cost(D)\ \leq\ \cost(B)\ +\ \cost(C),

and therefore

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ +\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).\qed

4.2
Bottleneck Distance of minimal projective resolutions

With matchings and
\dist_{\mathrm{R}}
in hand, we define the global distance by allowing padding.

Definition 4.6
:

For minimal projective resolutions
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
and
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}
(not necessarily of the same size), the
bottleneck distance
is

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ :=\ \inf_{\ (E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})}\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).

We adopt the extended-value convention that
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)=\infty
if there is no compatible padding (i.e.
\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=\varnothing
).

Remark 4.7
:

Let
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, and
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Then by using
\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
instead of
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,
it is possible to define a distance
\dist_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
between

E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
, and a distance
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
between

P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
, and we can prove the parallel statements for these
distances.
However, these distances are very coarse as Example
4.14
below shows.
Note that
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
if and only if there exist
a pair
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
with
the property that
\cost(B)=0
for some
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.

Proposition
4.3
gives a sufficient condition for finiteness (equality of alternating sums) of
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}
; in general,

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}
may be infinite.
However, as will be shown in Corollary
5.3
,

\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})
has some pair
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

such that
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\varnothing
(and hence
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})<\infty
)
whenever there exists a Galois coupling of
(M,N)
.

Example 4.8
:

There exists a finite poset
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{P}
-modules
M
and
N
such that

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=0
,
but
P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\not\cong P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
.
For example, let
\mathbf{P}:=\{1<2\}
,
M:=V_{\{1\}}\oplus V_{\{2\}}
, and

N:=V_{\mathbf{P}}=\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}
.
Then
P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}})

(as nonnegative complexes) are given as follows:

\displaystyle P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\dots\to 0\to\Bbbk[P]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 1)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}),\text{and}

\displaystyle F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\dots\to 0\to\Bbbk[P]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}).

Hence
(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},\,F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Definie
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},\,F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\deg&1&0\\
\hline\cr P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\ \ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\\
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\ \ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\\
\hline\cr\dist&0&0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\end{array}.

Then we have
\cost(B)=0
, and hence
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
.
However, it is clear that
P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\not\cong P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=(\dots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1})
.
Note that
B
above does not satisfy the compatibility on differentials.

We next give a criterion when
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
,
which gives a necessary condition
for
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
.
We denote by
K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})
the (split) Grothendieck group of the
category of projective
\mathbf{P}
-modules.
For each projective
\mathbf{P}
-module
P
, we denote by
[P]
the element
of
K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})
containing the isomorphism class of
P
.
Then the set
\{[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]\mid x\in\mathbf{P}\}
forms a basis of

K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})
.
For each nonnegative bounded complex
E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=(E_{i},\partial_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

of projective
\mathbf{P}
-modules, we set

[E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}]:=([E_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}
.
For any
A,B\in K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}
, we write

A\equiv B
if
A-B\in\langle[\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}})[i]]\mid x\in\mathbf{P},\,i\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
.

Lemma 4.9
:

Define a group homomorphism
\hat{\alpha}\colon K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}\to K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})
by
\hat{\alpha}((A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(-1)^{i}A_{i}

for all
(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}
.
Then
\operatorname{Ker}\hat{\alpha}=\langle[\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}})[i]]\mid x\in\mathbf{P},\,i\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
.
Therefore,
A\equiv B
if and only if
\hat{\alpha}(A)=\hat{\alpha}(B)
.

Proof.

(\supseteq)
.
Let
x\in\mathbf{P}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
We set
e_{i,x}:=[\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}})[i]]
for short.
Then

\hat{\alpha}(e_{i,x})=\hat{\alpha}(\dots,0,\overset{\begin{smallmatrix}(\deg\,i+1)\\
\ \end{smallmatrix}}{[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]},\overset{\begin{smallmatrix}(\deg\,i)\\
\ \end{smallmatrix}}{[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]},0,\dots,0)=(-1)^{i+1}[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]+(-1)^{i}[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]=0.

Hence
\operatorname{Ker}\hat{\alpha}\supseteq\langle e_{i,x}\mid x\in\mathbf{P},i\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
.
Set the right hand side of this to be
K
.

(\subseteq)
.
Let
A\in K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}
.
We define its
width

w(A)
by

w(A):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $A_{i}=0$ for all }i\in\mathbb{N},\\
\max\operatorname{supp}(A)-\min\operatorname{supp}(A)+1&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\operatorname{supp}(A):=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid A_{i}\neq 0\}
.
We show that if
A\in\operatorname{Ker}\hat{\alpha}
, then
A\in K
by induction on
w(A)
.

When
w(A)=0,1
, then obviously
A=0\in K
.
Therefore, assume
w(A)\geq 2
.
By definition of width, there exist integers
m,n
with
n>m\geq 0
such that

A=(\dots,0,A_{n},\dots,A_{m},0,\dots,0)\quad\text{with }A_{m},A_{n}\neq 0.

Using the basis of
K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})
given above, we can write

A_{m}=\sum_{x\in\mathbf{P}}a_{x}[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]=\sum_{x\in S}a_{x}[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}]

for some
(a_{x})_{x\in\mathbf{P}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{(\mathbf{P})}
, where
S:=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid a_{x}\neq 0\}

is a finite set.
Define
B\in K_{\operatorname{prj}}(\mathbf{P})^{(\mathbb{N})}
by

B:=A-\sum_{x\in S}a_{x}e_{m,x}=(\dots,0,A_{n},\dots,A_{m+1}-\sum_{x\in S}a_{x}[\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{x}],0,0,\dots,0).

Then
w(B)\leq w(A)-1
.
Moreover, we have
\hat{\alpha}(B)=0
by the linearity of
\hat{\alpha}
and
(\supseteq)
above.
Thus
B\in\operatorname{Ker}\hat{\alpha}
.
Therefore, by induction hypothesis, we have
B\in K
, and hence

A=B+\sum_{x\in S}a_{x}e_{m,x}\in K
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
:

(1) Let
E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
be nonnegative bounded complexes of projective
\mathbf{P}
-modules.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}^{\prime}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
,

(b)

There exists some
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
such that
\cost(B)=0
,

(c)

[E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}]=[F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}]
.

(2) Let
M,N
be
\mathbf{P}
-modules.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
,

(b)

\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
has a pair
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

such that
\cost(B)=0
for some
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,

(c)

[P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}]\equiv[P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}]
,

(d)

\hat{\alpha}([P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])=\hat{\alpha}([P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])
.

In particular, if
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=0
, then

\hat{\alpha}([P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])=\hat{\alpha}([P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])
.

Proof.

(1) is clear by definition.

(2) follows from (1) and Lemmas
4.1
and
4.9
.
∎

Theorem 4.11
:

The function
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}
defines an extended pseudometric on the set of
minimal projective resolutions
\{P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}\mid M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\}
.

Proof.

First, it holds that
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M})=0
for every
M
.
Indeed,
B:=(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{smd}(P^{M}_{i})})_{i\geq 0}
is a matching
of
(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, and
\cost(B)=0
.

Symmetry holds because any matching
B=(B_{i})
between two padded resolutions
induces an inverse matching
B^{-1}=(B_{i}^{-1})
of the same cost in the
opposite direction. Hence

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}).

To show the triangle inequality, let
M,N,O\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
We have to verify

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\ +\ \dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O}).

(4.4)

If
\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=\varnothing
or
\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O})=\varnothing
,
then the right hand side is infinity, and the inequality holds.
Therefore, we may assume that

\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\neq\varnothing
and
\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O})\neq\varnothing
.
If
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=\varnothing

for all
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})
, then again
the right hand is infinity.
Hence we may assume that
\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\varnothing

for some
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})
.
Similarly, we may assume that

\Match(F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\varnothing

for some
(F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O})
.
Let

\displaystyle(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

\displaystyle\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\text{ with }B\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}),\text{and }

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

\displaystyle\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O})\text{ with }B^{\prime}\in\Match(F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},O_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).

By Lemma
4.1
, both
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
have the forms

F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},\quad F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

for some
C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
that are finite direct sums of shifted cones
of the forms
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[a]
for some
E\in\operatorname{prj}\mathbf{P}
and
a\in\mathbb{N}
.
Set

E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},\,H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},\text{ and }G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}.

Then as is easily checked we have

(B_{i}\sqcup 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{smd}(C^{\prime}_{i})})_{i\geq 0}\in\Match(E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

and
(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{smd}(C_{i})}\sqcup B^{\prime}_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\Match(H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,
which are illustrated as

\operatorname{smd}(E_{i})\sqcup\operatorname{smd}(C^{\prime}_{i})\xrightarrow{B_{i}\sqcup 1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{smd}(C^{\prime}_{i})}}\operatorname{smd}(F_{i})\sqcup\operatorname{smd}(C^{\prime}_{i})=\operatorname{smd}(C_{i})\sqcup\operatorname{smd}(F^{\prime}_{i})\xrightarrow{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{smd}(C_{i})}\sqcup B^{\prime}_{i}}\operatorname{smd}(C_{i})\sqcup\operatorname{smd}(G_{i}).

Then since
|E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|+|C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
and
|G_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|+|C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
,
we have

|E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|+|C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|+|C^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|\ \ .

Thus

(E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}),\qquad(H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{O}).

Applying Lemma
4.5
yields

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ +\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(H_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},G^{*}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}).

Therefore (
4.4
) holds.
∎

4.3
Examples

We now compute bottleneck distances for our 1D and 2D running examples by
comparing minimal projective resolutions and equalizing degreewise sizes
via contractible cones.

Example 4.12
:

Let
\mathbf{P}=\{1<2<3<4\}
and consider

M=V_{[1,2)}\oplus V_{[2,4)},\qquad N=V_{[2,4)}

as in Example
3.9
.
Minimal projective resolutions (as nonnegative complexes)
of their summands are given as follows:

\displaystyle P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{V_{[1,2)}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\rho(2\geq 1)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}),

\displaystyle P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{V_{[2,4)}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\xrightarrow{\rho(4\geq 2)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}).

Summing yields

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\deg&1&0\\
\hline\cr P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\\
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\end{array}

with
|P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}|=(\dots,0,2,2)
and
|P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}|=(\dots,0,1,1)
. Since the alternating sums agree, padding is possible.
Pad
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}
by
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}})

to have

F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}})
,
adding
\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}
in both degrees
1
and
0
, which matches the size vector of
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
.
Define a matching
B\in\Match(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc}\deg&\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 0\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\\
\hline\cr F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\\
\hline\cr\dist&1&0&0&0\end{array}\ ,

where since we have

\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{P^{M}}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right],\text{ and }\ \operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right]

under the orders of summands as in the table,

B
satisfies the compatibility on differentials.
Thus
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\leq 1
.
Since
\hat{\alpha}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\hat{\alpha}(P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, it holds
by Lemma
4.10
(2) that

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=1.

Example 4.13
:

Let
\mathbf{P}=\{1,2,3\}^{2}
with the product order and
L^{\infty}
metric, and
consider the modules
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
from Example
3.12
.

As is easily seen (e.g., see
1
, Proposition 41
),

M_{1},\,M_{2}
and
N
have the following
minimal projective resolutions (as nonnegative complexes):

\displaystyle P^{M_{1}}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{23}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(23\geq 22)\\
-\rho(23\geq 13)\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{13}\xrightarrow{[\rho(22\geq 12)\ \rho(13\geq 12)]}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{12})

\displaystyle P^{M_{2}}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}0\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.58342pt}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}\xrightarrow{\rho(22\geq 21)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{21})

\displaystyle P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\xrightarrow{\hskip 16.38895pt}0\xrightarrow{\hskip 24.58342pt}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{33}\xrightarrow{\rho(33\geq 22)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22})

with
|P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}|=(\dots,0,1,3,2),|P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}|=(\dots,0,0,1,1)
.
Padding
P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
by

C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{12}})\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}})[1]

to have
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
,
we can define a pre-matching
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|ccc|cc}\deg&2&\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 0\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr P^{M_{1}}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{23}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{13}&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{12}&\\
P^{M_{2}}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&&&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{21}\\
\hline\cr P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&&&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{33}&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}\\
C_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{22}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{12}&&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{12}&\\
\hline\cr\dist&1&0&1&1&0&1\end{array}\ ,

where the matrices of differencials under the above orders of
summands are given by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}&\partial_{1}&\partial_{0}\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right]\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right]\end{array}

and hence
B
does not satisfy the compatibility condition on differentials
for
(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
However, we have the following commutative diagram,
where the vertical morphisms are isomorphisms (we omit
\rho(\cdots)
parts in the entries of matrices for simplicity):

\hbox to301.06pt{\vbox to77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 150.52773pt\lower-26.57838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Define a complex
F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
by the upper row in the diagram.
Then
\operatorname{pMatch}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=\operatorname{pMatch}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,
and since
F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\cong F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
, we have
(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, and the
B
above
satisfies the compatibility on differentials for
(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
,
and hence
B\in\Match(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F^{\prime}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, and
\cost(B)=1
.
Therefore
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\leq\cost(B)=1
. Since
\hat{\alpha}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\hat{\alpha}(P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, it holds
by Lemma
4.10
(2) that

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=1.

The following is a slightly simplified version of an example reported by
Luis Scoccola to the first version of the preprint.

Example 4.14
:

Let
\mathbf{P}:=\{1<2<3<4<5\}
, and let
M:=V_{[2,4]}
and
N:=0
.
Then

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=1.

(4.5)

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\dots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{5}\xrightarrow{\rho(5\geq 2)}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}),

\displaystyle P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\dots\to 0\to 0).

Let
E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}})\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}})
and

F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}})\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}})\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}})
.
Then we can define
B\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\deg&1&0\\
\hline\cr E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{5}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\\
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\\
\hline\cr\dist&1\hskip 20.00003pt1\hskip 20.00003pt1&0\hskip 20.00003pt0\hskip 20.00003pt0\end{array}.

Hence
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\leq\cost(B)=1
.
Since
\hat{\alpha}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\hat{\alpha}(P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, (
4.5
) holds
by Lemma
4.10
(2).

In this case, under these orders of summands, we have

\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{E}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\quad\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right],

and hence
B
above does not satisfy the compatibility of differentials.
If we define
B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{pMatch}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\deg&1&0\\
\hline\cr E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{5}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\\
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}&\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{3}\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{4}\\
\hline\cr\dist&3\hskip 20.00003pt0\hskip 20.00003pt0&0\hskip 20.00003pt0\hskip 20.00003pt0\end{array},

then
B^{\prime}\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
, and
\cost(B^{\prime})=3
.

Similarly, for
\mathbf{P}=\{1<2<\cdots<n\}
with
n\geq 2
,
we see that
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}^{\prime}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=1
for

M=V_{[a,b]}
and
N=0
for all
1\leq a\leq b<n
.

5
Stability Theorem

We now relate the two distances defined above. Informally: a Galois coupling of
M
and
N
controls, via restriction, a pair of projective resolutions whose degreewise summands can be matched with cost bounded by the coupling cost. Hence the bottleneck distance between minimal projective resolutions is at most the Galois transport distance.

The next lemma says that pulling back along the right adjoint of a Galois connection sends the indecomposable projective at
x\in\mathbf{Q}
to the indecomposable projective at
f(x)\in\mathbf{P}
.

Lemma 5.1
:

If
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
is a Galois connection of posets, then
for each
x\in\mathbf{Q}
, we have an isomorphism

g^{*}\bigl(\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{x}\bigr)\ \cong\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{f(x)}\qquad\text{in }\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}

that is natural in
x
.

Proof.

For
y\in\mathbf{P}
, the value of
g^{*}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}](x,\operatorname{-})
at
y
is

(g^{*}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}](x,\operatorname{-}))(y)=\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}](x,g(y))\cong\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](f(x),y)

natural in
x
and
y
because
\mathbf{Q}(x,g(y))\cong\mathbf{P}(f(x),y)
.
Hence
g^{*}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}](x,\operatorname{-})\cong\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}](f(x),\operatorname{-})
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
∎

Theorem 5.2

(Stability)
:

Let
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
be a finite metric poset. Then for any
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
,

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N).

Proof.

If there is no Galois coupling of
(M,N)
, then
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=\infty
and the claim is tautological. Otherwise fix
\varepsilon>0
and choose a coupling
(\mathbf{Q},f\dashv g,\ h\dashv i,\ \Gamma)
with

g^{\ast}\Gamma\cong M,\qquad i^{\ast}\Gamma\cong N,\qquad\cost(\Gamma)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)+\varepsilon.

Let
R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\to\Gamma
be any projective resolution in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
. Since precomposition is exact (Proposition
2.8
) and, for a Galois connection, preserves projectives (Proposition
2.16
), the complexes

E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=g^{*}R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}:=i^{*}R_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

are projective resolutions of
M
and
N
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.

By Lemmas
2.4
and
2.6
,
we may set

\displaystyle R_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{x},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{R}_{i})

\displaystyle=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\partial^{R}_{i}

\displaystyle=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}\,\rho(x^{(i+1)}\geq x^{(i)})]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\colon R_{i+1}\to R_{i}.

By Lemma
5.1
(and the analogue for
h\dashv i
), we have

\displaystyle E_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{f(x)},\quad F_{i}=\bigoplus_{x\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{h(x)},

\displaystyle\partial^{E}_{i}

\displaystyle=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}\,\rho(f(x^{(i+1)})\geq f(x^{(i)}))]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})},

\displaystyle\partial^{F}_{i}

\displaystyle=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}\,\rho(h(x^{(i+1)})\geq h(x^{(i)}))]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}.

Here note that the following holds:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{E}_{i})&=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})},\\
\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{i})&=[a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}]_{(x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)})\,\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i+1})\times\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})}.\end{aligned}\right.

(5.1)

Indeed, if
a^{(i)}_{x^{(i+1)},x^{(i)}}\neq 0
, then

x^{(i+1)}\geq x^{(i)}
, which shows that both

f(x^{(i+1)})\geq f(x^{(i)})
and
h(x^{(i+1)})\geq h(x^{(i)})
,
as desired.

Hence
|E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|=|F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}|
and
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})
. Define the degreewise bijection
B_{i}
by the identity on indices
x\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})
:

B_{i}:\ \Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{f(x)}\longmapsto\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{h(x)}\qquad(x\in\operatorname{smd}(R_{i})).

Then by (
5.1
),
B=(B_{i})_{i\geq 0}
satisfies the compatibility condition on
differentials, and hence
B
is a matching of
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
.
Here we have

\dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ \leq\ \cost(B)\ =\ \sup_{i}\ \sup_{x\in S_{i}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\bigl(f(x),h(x)\bigr)\ \leq\ \sup_{x\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}\bigl(f(x),h(x)\bigr)\ =\ \cost(\Gamma).

Taking the infimum over all compatible paddings yields

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{R}}(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\ \leq\ \cost(\Gamma)\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)+\varepsilon.

This completes the proof.
∎

Note that in the proof of Theorem
5.2
, the following was proved.

Corollary 5.3
:

For any
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, if
there exists a Galois coupling of
(M,N)
, then we have

\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\varnothing
, and moreover,
there exists some
(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\in\mathsf{Res}(P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
such that

\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})\neq\varnothing.

The following gives an illustration of our proof of
Theorem
5.2
.

Example 5.4
:

We compute the bottleneck distance of the minimal projective
resolutions of the modules
M
and
N
in
Example
3.14
.
We first compute a minimal projective resolution of

\Gamma=V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathbf{Q}}\oplus V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\{1_{L}\}}
.
A minimal projective resolutions of
V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathbf{Q}}
and

V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\{1_{L}\}}
are given as follows:

\displaystyle 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 1_{L})\\
-\rho(2\geq 1_{R})\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{L}}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{R}}\xrightarrow{[\rho(1_{L})\ \rho(1_{R})]}

\displaystyle V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\mathbf{Q}}\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\rho(2\geq 1_{L})}\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{L}}\xrightarrow{\rho(1_{L})}

\displaystyle V^{\mathbf{Q}}_{\{1_{L}\}}\to 0.

Therefore, a minimal projective resolution
P^{\Gamma}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
of
\Gamma

as a nonnegative complex is given by

\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 1_{L})&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 1_{R})&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 1_{L})\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\overbrace{\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{L}}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{R}}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{Q}]_{1_{L}}}^{\deg 0}.

Up to the natural isomorphism stated in Lemma
5.1
,
the exact functors
g^{*}
and
i^{*}
send this to the following, which are projective resolutions of
M
and
N
, respectively:

\displaystyle E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle:=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 1)&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 1)\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\overbrace{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}}^{\deg 0}),

(5.2)

\displaystyle\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\mapsto$}\hskip 30.0pt\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\mapsto$}\phantom{\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(1\leq 2)&0\\
-\rho(2\leq 2)&0\\
0&\rho(1\leq 2)\end{smallmatrix}\right]}}\hskip 30.0pt\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\mapsto$}\hskip 30.0pt\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\mapsto$}\hskip 30.0pt\rotatebox{-90.0}{$\mapsto$}

\displaystyle F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle:=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 1)&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 2)\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\underbrace{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{1}\oplus\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}}_{\deg 0}).

Since
\rho(2\geq 1)\circ\rho(2\geq 2)=\rho(\pi_{2\geq 2}\pi_{2\geq 1})=\rho(2\geq 1)
, an elementary row operation shows that
the matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 1)&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 1)\end{smallmatrix}\right]
in
E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
is equivalent to
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 1)\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, and
the matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
-\rho(2\geq 1)&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 2)\end{smallmatrix}\right]
in
F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

is equivalent to
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\rho(2\geq 2)&0\\
0&0\\
0&\rho(2\geq 2)\end{smallmatrix}\right]
,
which means that

\displaystyle E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle\cong P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}}),\ \text{and}

\displaystyle F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle\cong P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}})\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\mathbf{P}]_{2}}).

Define a matching
B\in\Match(E_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})

vertically as displayed in (
5.2
).
Then clearly,
\cost(B)=1
, and hence
we have
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)\leq 1
.
Since
\hat{\alpha}([P^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])\neq\hat{\alpha}([P^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}])
, we have

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)>0
by
Lemma
4.10
.
Therefore,
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}\bigl(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}\bigr)=1
.

5.1
Examples

We now revisit our running 1D and 2D examples to illustrate the stability
inequality.
In both cases the Galois transport distance and the bottleneck distance
coincide, showing that the bound in Theorem
5.2
is sharp.

Example 5.5
:

From Examples
3.9
and
4.12
we have

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)=1,\qquad\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=1.

Hence the stability inequality

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}(M,N)

holds with equality.

On the transport side, the Galois coupling moves each interval in
M
forward
by one step in the parameter. On the resolution side,
padding by
contractible cones aligns the minimal projective resolutions and produces a matching of the same cost.
Thus stability is numerically sharp in this one-parameter example.

Example 5.6
:

For the
2
-parameter modules
M
and
N
from Example
3.12
, we
computed

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})=1

in Examples
3.12
and
4.13
. Hence

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\ =\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)\ =\ 1,

so the stability inequality holds with equality.

Geometrically, the transport coupling shifts each subspace of
M
by

(+1,+1)
or
(0,+1)
into the corresponding arm of the
L
-shape
N
.
On the resolution
side,
padding
P_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N}
by two shifted cones equalizes the degreewise sizes,
and permits a matching of cost
1
. Stability is therefore sharp in this
two-parameter example as well.

6
Application to Persistence

In this section we extract a persistence-like construction from a

\mathbf{P}
-module by passing to the interval poset and taking kernels of structure
maps. We then show that the Galois transport stability inequality descends to
this construction, recovering classical bottleneck stability when
\mathbf{P}
is a
finite chain.

Definition 6.1

(Augmented poset)
:

Let
(\mathbf{P},d_{\mathbf{P}})
be a finite metric poset,
\top
a symbol
not contained in
\mathbf{P}
.
We set
\overline{\mathbf{P}}:=\mathbf{P}\cup\{\top\}
, and define a partial order
on it by extending that of
\mathbf{P}
with additional order

x<\top
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
and
\top\leq\top
.
If
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
are distinct finite posets,
then we treat the top elements
\top
of
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
are distinct
although we denote them by the same symbol.
If we need to distinguish the top element,
then we denote it by
\top_{\mathbf{P}}
for
\mathbf{P}
.

We extend
d_{\mathbf{P}}
to an
extended
metric

d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\overline{\mathbf{P}}\to[0,+\infty]
by

d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(x,\top)=d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\top,x)=+\infty\ (x\neq\top),\qquad d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\top,\top)=0,

which defines a finite metric poset
(\overline{\mathbf{P}},d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}})
.

We extend this correspondence
\mathbf{P}\mapsto\overline{\mathbf{P}}

to a 2-functor
\overline{(\operatorname{-})}\colon\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}
of the 2-category
\mathbf{Pos}
of finite posets.
If
f\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
is a monotone map, then we define a monotone
map
\overline{f}\colon\overline{\mathbf{P}}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
to be an extension of
f
with

\overline{f}(\top):=\top
.

If
f,g\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
are monotone maps as functors, and

\alpha\colon f\Rightarrow g
is a natural transformation, then we define
a natural transformation
\overline{\alpha}\colon\overline{f}\Rightarrow\overline{g}
in an obvious way.
Namely, the fact that
\alpha\colon f\Rightarrow g
is a natural transformation
means that
f(x)\leq g(x)
for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
and
\alpha_{x}:=\pi_{g(x)\geq f(x)}\colon f(x)\to g(x)
in
\mathbf{Q}
(see (
2.1
) for the notation of
\pi
), which implies that

\overline{f}(x)\leq\overline{g}(x)
for all
x\in\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, and therefore,
we can set
\overline{\alpha}_{x}:=\pi_{\overline{g}(x)\geq\overline{f}(x)}\colon\overline{f}(x)\to\overline{g}(x)
in
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
to define a natural transformation

\overline{\alpha}:=(\overline{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\colon\overline{f}\Rightarrow\overline{g}
.

We will apply the following definition to the finite poset
\overline{\mathbf{P}}

for a finite poset
\mathbf{P}
.
In order to avoid confusion, we use other letters
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{T}

to denote finite posets in this definition.

Definition 6.2
:

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a finite metric poset.
Define the
interval poset

\Int\mathbf{S}
to be the
full subposet of the product poset
\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{S}
whose unnderlying set is given by the graph of the binary relation
\leq
on
\mathbf{S}
, thus

\Int\mathbf{S}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{S}\mid x\leq y\}.

(6.1)

Equip
\Int\mathbf{S}
with the
L^{\infty}
extended metric

d_{\Int\mathbf{S}}\bigl((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\bigr):=\max\{\,d_{\mathbf{S}}(x_{1},x_{2}),\,d_{\mathbf{S}}(y_{1},y_{2})\,\}

to define a finite metric poset
(\Int\mathbf{S},d_{\Int\mathbf{S}})
.

Remark 6.3
:

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a finite poset.
Then the correspondence

(x,y)\mapsto[x,y]
gives an isomorphism
from
\Int\mathbf{S}
to the poset
\{[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathbf{S},x\leq y\}
of

closed intervals
in
\mathbf{S}
whose partial order is defined by

[x_{1},y_{1}]\leq[x_{2},y_{2}]\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x_{1}\leq x_{2}\ \text{and}\ y_{1}\leq y_{2}.

By this similarity, we adopt the notation
\Int
standing for intervals.
But note that this order is not given by the inclusion relation of intervals
as in other literature such as
[
3
]
.

Definition 6.4
:

We extend the correspondence
\mathbf{S}\mapsto\Int\mathbf{S}

to a 2-functor
\Int\colon\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}
.
Let
f\colon\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{T}
be a monotone map.
We define
\Int f\colon\Int\mathbf{S}\to\Int\mathbf{T}
by setting

(\Int f)((x,y)):=(f(x),f(y))
for all
(x,y)\in\Int\mathbf{S}
.
Note here that
(f(x),f(y))\in\Int\mathbf{S}
because
f
is monotone.
If
(x_{1},y_{1})\leq(x_{2},y_{2})
in
\Int\mathbf{S}
, then
we have
(f(x_{1}),f(y_{1}))\leq(f(x_{2}),f(y_{2}))
again because
f
is monotone.
Hence we can define

(\Int f)(\pi_{(x_{2},y_{2})\geq(x_{1},y_{1})}):=\pi_{(f(x_{2}),f(y_{2}))\geq(f(x_{1}),(y_{1}))}.

Finally let
\alpha\colon f\Rightarrow g
be a natural transformation
between monotone maps
f,g\colon\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{T}
regarded as functors.
Then for each
x\in\mathbf{S}
,
\alpha_{x}\colon f(x)\to g(x)
means that

f(x)\leq g(x)
in
\mathbf{T}
and
\alpha_{x}=\pi_{g(x)\geq f(x)}
.
Then for any
(x,y)\in\Int\mathbf{S}
, we have

(f(x),f(y))\leq(g(x),g(y))
in
\Int\mathbf{T}
.
Hence we can set

(\Int\alpha)_{(x,y)}:=\pi_{[g(x),g(y))\geq[f(x),f(y))}\colon(\Int f)((x,y))\to(\Int g)((x,y)),

which defines a natural transformation

\Int\alpha:=((\Int\alpha)_{[x,y)})_{[x,y)\in\Int\mathbf{S}}\colon\Int f\Rightarrow\Int g
.

Remark 6.5
:

To define
\Int f\colon\Int\mathbf{S}\to\Int\mathbf{T}
for a monotone map

f\colon\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{T}
of finite posets,
we need to have
(z,z)\in\Int\mathbf{T}
for all
z\in\mathbf{T}
because it may occur
that
f(x)=f(y)
even if
x<y
in
\mathbf{S}
.
Therefore, in (
6.1
), we cannot replace
x\leq y
by
x<y
.
Thus even if
\mathbf{S}
is a finite linearly ordered set,
we cannot replace
\Int\mathbf{S}
by the poset
\{[x,y)\mid x,y\in\mathbf{S},x\leq y\}
of half-open intervals in
\mathbf{S}
whose partial order is defined by the same was as in the
closed interval case in Remark
6.3
.

By composing these 2-functors,
we obtain a 2-functor

\Int\circ\overline{(\operatorname{-})}\colon\mathbf{Pos}\to\mathbf{Pos}.

(6.2)

This 2-functor is used in the proof of Lemma
6.8
.

Definition 6.6
:

We define a functor
\operatorname{ext}\colon\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
as follows.
Let
M
be in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then
\operatorname{ext}(M):=\overline{M}\colon\overline{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}
is defined by
extending
M
with
\overline{M}(\top):=0
and
\overline{M}(\pi_{\top\geq x}):=0

for all
x\in\mathbf{P}
.

Let
\beta\colon M\to N
be a morphism in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then
\operatorname{ext}(\beta):=\overline{\beta}\colon\overline{M}\to\overline{N}
is defined
by extending
\beta
with
\overline{\beta}_{\top}:=0\colon\overline{M}(\top)\to\overline{N}(\top)
.

Definition 6.7
:

Let
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then we define
K(M)\in\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
as follows.
For each
(x,y)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, we set

K(M)((x,y)):=\operatorname{Ker}\overline{M}(\pi_{y\geq x}).

Note that we have
K(M)((x,\top))=M(x)

by the definition of
\overline{M}
.
Next let
(x_{1},y_{1})\leq(x_{2},y_{2})
in
\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)((x_{1},y_{1}))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(x_{1})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(y_{1})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)((x_{2},y_{2}))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(x_{2})}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(y_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{y_{1}\geq x_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{y_{2}\geq x_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)(\pi_{(x_{2},y_{2})\geq(x_{1},y_{1})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{x_{2}\geq x_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{y_{2}\geq y_{1}})}

with exact rows (first ignore the dashed arrow).
By the universality of the kernel, there exists a unique morphism

K(M)(\pi_{(x_{2},y_{2})\geq(x_{1},y_{1})})
making the diagram
commutative, which is a restriction of
\overline{M}(\pi_{x_{2}\geq x_{1}})
.
As is easily seen, the above defines a functor

K(M)\colon\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}
, an object of
\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
.

We extend this to a functor
K:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
.
Let
\beta\colon M\to N
be in
\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then we define a morphism
K(\beta):=(K(\beta)_{(x,y)})_{(x,y)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\colon K(M)\to K(N)
in
\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}

as follows.
For each
(x,y)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, we have a commutative diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)((x,y))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(x)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(y)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(N)((x,y))}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{N}(x)}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{N}(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{y\geq x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}(\pi_{y\geq x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\beta)_{(x,y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{y}}

with exact rows.
Again the universality of the kernel yields a unique morphism

K(\beta)_{(x,y)}
above
making the diagram commutative,
which is just the restriction of
\beta_{x}
to
K(M)((x,y))
.
Then it is easy to see that

K:\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
becomes a functor.
When we need to distinguish
\mathbf{P}
, we denote it by
K_{\mathbf{P}}
.

We record two lemmas that will be used below.

Lemma 6.8
:

If
f:\mathbf{Q}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{P}:g
is a Galois connection (resp. insertion), then so is

\Int\overline{f}:\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}\rightleftarrows\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}:\Int\overline{g}.

Proof.

Apply the 2-functor
\Int\circ\overline{(\operatorname{-})}
defined
in (
6.2
) to an adjoint system

(f,g,\eta,\varepsilon)
to obtain an adjoint system

(\Int\overline{f},\Int\overline{g},\Int\overline{\eta},\Int\overline{\varepsilon})
.
Here for insersions, note that
if
f\circ g=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathbf{P}}
, then
(\Int\overline{f})\circ(\Int\overline{g})=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
:

For any monotone maps
f,h:\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbf{P}
, we have

\sup_{(u,v)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\bigl((\Int\overline{f})(u,v),(\Int\overline{h})(u,v)\bigr)\ \leq\ \sup_{w\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(w),\overline{h}(w)).

Proof.

For any
(u,v)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
, we have

d_{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\bigl((\Int\overline{f})(u,v),(\Int\overline{h})(u,v)\bigr)=\max\{d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(u),\overline{h}(u)),d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(v),\overline{h}(v))\}\leq\sup_{w\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(w),\overline{h}(w)),

from which the inequality follows.
∎

Lemma 6.10
:

For any monotone map
g:\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
,
we have a strictly commutative diagram

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Int\overline{g})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mathbf{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\mathbf{P}}}

of functors:

(\Int\overline{g})^{*}\circ K_{\mathbf{Q}}=K_{\mathbf{P}}\circ g^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{Q}}
, and
(x,y)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then

\displaystyle((\Int\overline{g})^{*}\circ K_{Q})(M)((x,y))

\displaystyle=K_{Q}(M)((\overline{g}(x),\overline{g}(y)))=\operatorname{Ker}\overline{M}(\pi_{\overline{g}(y)\geq\overline{g}(x)}),

\displaystyle(K_{\mathbf{P}}\circ g^{*})(M)((x,y))

\displaystyle=K_{\mathbf{P}}(M\circ g)((x,y))=\operatorname{Ker}(\overline{M\circ g})(\pi_{y\geq x})=\operatorname{Ker}\overline{M}(\pi_{\overline{g}(y)\geq\overline{g}(x)}).

Hence
(\Int\overline{g})^{*}\circ K_{\mathbf{Q}}=K_{\mathbf{P}}\circ g^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
:

Let
(\mathbf{Q},\,f\dashv g,\,h\dashv i,\,\Gamma)
be a Galois coupling of the pair
(M,N)
.
Then

(\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\,\Int\overline{f}\dashv\Int\overline{g},\,\Int\overline{h}\dashv\Int\overline{i},\,K(\Gamma))

is a Galois coupling of the pair
(K(M),K(N))
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
,
we have Galois insertions
\Int\overline{f}\dashv\Int\overline{g}

and
\Int\overline{h}\dashv\Int\overline{i}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
6.10
, we have

K(M)=K(g^{*}(\Gamma))=(\Int\overline{g})^{*}(K(\Gamma))
, and similarly,

K(N)=(\Int\overline{i})^{*}(K(\Gamma))
.
∎

Proposition 6.12
:

For all
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, we have

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(K(M),K(N))\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N).

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{Q},\,f\dashv g,\,h\dashv i,\,\Gamma)
be a Galois coupling of the pair
(M,N)
.
Then by Lemma
6.11
,

(\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\,\Int\overline{f}\dashv\Int\overline{g},\,\Int\overline{h}\dashv\Int\overline{i},\,K(\Gamma))

is a Galois coupling of the pair
(K(M),K(N))
.
Therefore, by Lemma
6.9
,

\cost K(\Gamma)\leq\cost\Gamma
.
Indeed,

\cost K(\Gamma)=\sup_{(u,v)\in\Int\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}\bigl((\Int\overline{f})(u,v),(\Int\overline{h})(u,v)\bigr)\ \leq\ \sup_{w\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(w),\overline{h}(w)).

Here if
w=\top\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
,
then we have

d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(w),\overline{h}(w))=d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(\top),\overline{h}(\top))=d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\top,\top)=0.

This shows that

\sup_{w\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}}d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\overline{f}(w),\overline{h}(w))=\sup_{w\in\mathbf{Q}}d_{\mathbf{P}}(f(w),h(w))=\cost\Gamma
.
Hence

\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(K(M),K(N))\leq\cost K(\Gamma)\leq\cost\Gamma,

which shows the assertion.
∎

Definition 6.13
:

For
M\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
let
K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M}
denote a minimal projective resolution of

K(M)
. Its degreewise indecomposable projective summands form the

persistence diagram
of
M
.

Padding a resolution by a cone
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{E})[a]
adds one copy of
E
in
degrees
a
and
a+1
, playing the role of adding diagonal points in classical
bottleneck matchings.

Theorem 6.14
:

For all
M,N\in\mathsf{vec}^{\mathbf{P}}
, we have

\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{M},K_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}^{N})\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(K(M),K(N))\ \leq\ \dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N).

For
\mathbf{P}=\{1<\dots<n\}
, this recovers classical bottleneck stability.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
5.2
to the finite metric poset

(\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}},d_{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}})
and
K(M),K(N)\in\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
to
have the first inequality, the second is stated in
Proposition
6.12
.
∎

6.1
Examples

We now compute persistence diagrams for our running 1D and 2D examples, and verify stability at the level of minimal resolutions in
\mathsf{vec}^{\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}}
.

Example 6.15
:

Let
\mathbf{P}
be as in Example
3.9
.
Then
\overline{\mathbf{P}}=\{1<2<3<4<\top\}
with
d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(x,y)=|x-y|
and

d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(x,\top)=\infty
for all
x,y\in\mathbf{P}
and
d_{\overline{\mathbf{P}}}(\top,\top)=0
.
Take the modules

M=V_{[1,1]}\oplus V_{[2,3]},\qquad N=V_{[2,3]}

as in that example.
Then
\overline{M},\overline{N}
are given as representations of
H(\overline{\mathbf{P}})=1\to 2\to 3\to 4\to\top
as follows by Definition
6.6
:

\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(2,3)}}
\displaystyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(3,4)}}

Therefore,
K^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
and
K^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
is given by
(differentials are given by their matrices)

\displaystyle K^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,2)}\oplus\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(4,4)}\xrightarrow{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}\oplus\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,4)}),

\displaystyle K^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}

\displaystyle=(\cdots\to 0\to\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(4,4)}\xrightarrow{1}\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,4)}).

Padding
K^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}
by
\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}})
to have

F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}=K^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}\oplus\Cone(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}})
,
we can give a matching
B\in\Match(K^{m}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})
by the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc}\deg&\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 0\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr K^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,2)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(4,4)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,4)}\\
\hline\cr F_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(4,4)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(1,2)}&\Bbbk[\Int\overline{\mathbf{P}}]_{(2,4)}\\
\hline\cr\dist&1&0&0&0\end{array}\ ,

where we have

\operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{K^{M}}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right],\text{ and }\ \operatorname{Mat}(\partial^{F}_{0})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right],

and hence
B
satisfies the compatibility condition on differentials.
Thus
\dist_{\mathrm{B}}(K^{M}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}},K^{N}_{\raisebox{1.0pt}{\scalebox{0.4}{$\bullet$}}})=1
.
Combined with
\dist_{\mathrm{GT}}^{\mathbf{P}}(M,N)=1
, this realizes the stability bound with equality.
